## 0.41 Cardiac Ectopic Beats (PVCs, PACs)

> [!note] Gap-filled from CSV (Cardiology category, Medium yield) — not covered as a standalone topic in the source UK notes.

**D:** Premature depolarisations arising outside the normal sinus node pathway. Premature ventricular contractions (PVCs) arise from an ectopic ventricular focus; premature atrial contractions (PACs) arise from an ectopic atrial focus.

**R:** caffeine, alcohol, stimulant drugs, electrolyte disturbance (↓K, ↓Mg), stress/anxiety, sleep deprivation, structural heart disease (more relevant for frequent/high-burden PVCs), hyperthyroidism, sympathomimetic drugs.

**A/P:** an ectopic focus depolarises earlier than the next expected sinus impulse → premature beat, usually followed by a compensatory pause (more complete after a PVC than a PAC, since PACs can reset the sinus node).

**S/Smx:** often asymptomatic and detected incidentally on ECG/Holter monitoring; may cause a sensation of a "skipped beat," fluttering, or a forceful subsequent beat (from the increased stroke volume of the post-ectopic beat). PVCs: wide, bizarre QRS not preceded by a P wave. PACs: an early, abnormally-shaped P wave followed by a usually-normal QRS (may be aberrantly conducted).

**Ix:** ECG — *why:* confirms the diagnosis and characterises morphology (helps localise the ectopic focus and distinguish benign from concerning patterns); *what:* premature wide QRS without preceding P wave (PVC) or premature abnormal P wave (PAC). Holter monitor — *why:* quantifies burden (ectopic beats as % of total beats over 24h) when frequent/symptomatic, since high burden (generally >10-20% of beats) can itself cause a reversible cardiomyopathy; *what:* establishes ectopic burden and any associated non-sustained arrhythmia. Bloods (U&E including Mg, TFT) — *why:* screens for reversible electrolyte/endocrine triggers; *what:* hypokalaemia, hypomagnesaemia, or thyrotoxicosis as contributors. Echo — *why:* to exclude underlying structural heart disease, particularly if PVCs are frequent/high-burden or symptomatic; *what:* usually normal in idiopathic ectopy; may reveal cardiomyopathy in structural disease.

**Mx:**
- **Immediate:** none required for isolated, infrequent, asymptomatic ectopics with a structurally normal heart.
- **Definitive:** correct any identified reversible trigger (reduce caffeine/alcohol/stimulants, correct electrolytes, treat hyperthyroidism). Reassurance for benign, low-burden ectopy. β-blockers first-line if symptomatic despite trigger avoidance. Catheter ablation considered for high-burden, symptomatic PVCs refractory to medical therapy, or if PVC-induced cardiomyopathy develops.
- **Chronic/long-term:** periodic reassessment (Holter ± echo) if burden is high, to monitor for the development of PVC-induced cardiomyopathy.

**P:** excellent in the absence of structural heart disease and with low ectopic burden — a normal variant in most people. High-burden PVCs (particularly sustained >10-20% of total beats) carry a small but real risk of causing a reversible dilated cardiomyopathy if untreated, which typically improves with successful suppression/ablation.

---

## 0.42 Restrictive Cardiomyopathy

> [!note] Gap-filled from CSV (Cardiology category, Medium yield) — dilated cardiomyopathy and HOCM are covered in the source notes but restrictive cardiomyopathy is not.

**D:** Cardiomyopathy characterised by rigid, non-compliant ventricular walls causing impaired diastolic filling, with typically preserved systolic function (until late disease) and normal or near-normal wall thickness/chamber size (distinguishing it from HOCM and DCM respectively).

**Causes:** infiltrative (amyloidosis — most common cause in developed countries, sarcoidosis, haemochromatosis), storage diseases (Fabry disease), endomyocardial (endomyocardial fibrosis, Loeffler endocarditis/hypereosinophilic syndrome), idiopathic, post-radiation fibrosis.

**A/P:** infiltration or fibrosis of the myocardium → stiff, non-compliant ventricles → impaired diastolic filling with a rapid early rise in filling pressure → elevated atrial pressures and biatrial enlargement → predominantly right and left heart failure symptoms with relatively preserved ejection fraction until late in the disease course.

**S/Smx:** heart failure symptoms — dyspnoea, fatigue, exercise intolerance; signs of right heart failure often prominent (↑JVP with rapid x and y descent, ascites, peripheral oedema, hepatomegaly); S3 or S4; features of the underlying infiltrative disease may be present (e.g. macroglossia, periorbital purpura in amyloidosis; skin/joint findings in sarcoidosis; bronze skin, diabetes in haemochromatosis).

**Ix:** ECG — *why:* screens for low-voltage QRS which is characteristic (and helps distinguish from LVH-causing conditions) plus arrhythmia/conduction disease common in infiltrative disease; *what:* low voltage (classically in amyloidosis, despite echo showing thickened walls — a discordance that's a diagnostic clue), conduction abnormalities. Echo — *why:* assesses diastolic function, wall thickness, and chamber size, and screens for the "granular sparkling" appearance of amyloid; *what:* restrictive filling pattern, biatrial enlargement, preserved EF, normal/mildly increased wall thickness. Cardiac MRI — *why:* better differentiates restrictive cardiomyopathy from constrictive pericarditis (an important clinical mimic) and characterises infiltrative tissue; *what:* late gadolinium enhancement pattern suggestive of amyloid/sarcoid infiltration. Endomyocardial biopsy — *why:* definitive tissue diagnosis when non-invasive workup is inconclusive; *what:* confirms the specific infiltrative process. Additional targeted Ix per suspected cause (e.g. serum/urine electrophoresis and fat pad biopsy for amyloid, ferritin/genetic testing for haemochromatosis, ACE level for sarcoidosis).

> [!info] Key differential: Restrictive Cardiomyopathy vs Constrictive Pericarditis
> Both cause a restrictive filling pattern, but the underlying pathology (and management) differs completely — constrictive pericarditis is a pericardial disease (surgically correctable via pericardiectomy), while restrictive cardiomyopathy is myocardial (usually not). MRI and cardiac catheterisation (looking at discordance between LV and RV pressures during respiration) are key to distinguishing them; misdiagnosis matters because constrictive pericarditis is potentially curable with surgery.

**Mx:**
- **Immediate/acute (decompensated HF):** diuretics for congestion — used cautiously, as restrictive physiology is preload-dependent and over-diuresis can drop cardiac output.
- **Definitive:** treat the underlying cause where possible (e.g. disease-modifying therapy for amyloidosis — e.g. tafamidis for transthyretin amyloidosis, or chemotherapy for light-chain amyloidosis; venesection for haemochromatosis; immunosuppression for sarcoidosis).
- **Chronic/long-term:** heart failure symptom management as per chronic HF (see section 0.27), with caution around standard HFrEF drug titration since these patients are often preload-dependent and intolerant of vasodilators/negative inotropes; rate control (avoiding aggressive negative chronotropes) if AF develops (common, given atrial enlargement); consider anticoagulation given AF/stasis risk from the enlarged, poorly-contracting atria; heart transplant for refractory end-stage disease in selected patients.

**P:** generally poor, especially for infiltrative causes such as amyloidosis, unless the underlying disease is identified early and disease-modifying therapy is available; significantly better if a treatable secondary cause (e.g. haemochromatosis) is identified and treated early.



<!-- ===== SOURCE: B1_Chest_Pain_Framework_and_Cardiac_Biomarkers.md ===== -->


> [!warning] Sourcing
> Written from model knowledge, not retrieved from guidelines. Differential reasoning, discriminators and interpretive logic are reliable at intern level. **Every threshold, time target, score cut-off, dose and reference range carries an `UNVERIFIED` marker naming what to check, or has been omitted with the omission stated in place.** Verify against current **Heart Foundation / Cardiac Society of Australia and New Zealand (CSANZ)** acute coronary syndrome guidance, eTG Cardiovascular, and your health network's chest pain pathway — accelerated diagnostic protocols and troponin cut-offs are assay- and site-specific.

---

## 0.1 Acute Chest Pain

**D:** New chest discomfort requiring exclusion of the conditions that kill quickly before consideration of the many that do not.

> [!danger] The six that must not be missed
> **Acute coronary syndrome · pulmonary embolism · aortic dissection · tension pneumothorax · oesophageal rupture (Boerhaave) · cardiac tamponade.**
> All six can present with chest pain and a benign-looking patient. The assessment is structured around excluding these first, and only then considering musculoskeletal, gastro-oesophageal and anxiety-related causes.

**A/P:** Visceral afferents from the heart, great vessels, oesophagus and mediastinum converge on the same spinal segments as somatic afferents from the chest wall and arm. **This shared innervation is why cardiac pain is poorly localised, radiates to the arm, jaw and epigastrium, and is genuinely indistinguishable from oesophageal pain by character alone** — the overlap is anatomical, not a failure of history-taking.

> [!warning] The classical descriptors discriminate less well than taught
> "Crushing, central, radiating to the left arm" raises the probability of ACS but its absence does not lower it usefully. Pain that is **sharp, positional, pleuritic or reproducible on palpation reduces the likelihood of ACS but does not exclude it** — a meaningful minority of confirmed infarcts have one of these features. Relief with GTN does not confirm cardiac pain (it relieves oesophageal spasm), and relief with antacid does not exclude it.
> **Do not use response to treatment as a diagnostic test in chest pain.**

> [!danger] Groups who present atypically
> **Women, patients with diabetes, the elderly, and patients with chronic kidney disease** more often present without chest pain at all — with dyspnoea, fatigue, nausea, syncope, epigastric discomfort or simply feeling unwell. **Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander people develop ischaemic heart disease at substantially younger ages and with worse outcomes**, so age-based reassurance is less safe in this group and the threshold for investigation should be lower.

> [!tip] Features pointing to each of the six
> | Diagnosis | Suggestive features |
> |---|---|
> | **ACS** | Exertional, heavy, radiation to arm/jaw, diaphoresis, nausea, risk factors |
> | **PE** | Pleuritic, sudden, dyspnoea, syncope, hypoxia, calf pain, immobility or malignancy |
> | **Aortic dissection** | **Tearing, maximal at onset, interscapular**, pulse or BP differential, new aortic regurgitation, neurological deficit |
> | **Tension pneumothorax** | Sudden, dyspnoea, hypotension, absent breath sounds, tracheal deviation (late) |
> | **Oesophageal rupture** | **After forceful vomiting**, severe pain, surgical emphysema, systemic toxicity |
> | **Tamponade** | Dyspnoea, hypotension, raised JVP, muffled sounds, pulsus paradoxus |

> [!danger] Aortic dissection — the diagnosis that gets anticoagulated by mistake
> Dissection presenting with ST elevation from coronary ostial involvement, or with chest pain and an abnormal ECG, has been treated as ACS — and **antiplatelet therapy, anticoagulation and thrombolysis are catastrophic in dissection.**
> Think of it with: pain **maximal at onset** rather than building · tearing or ripping quality · interscapular or migrating pain · **blood pressure differential between arms** · pulse deficit · new aortic regurgitation murmur · focal neurological deficit · syncope · a widened mediastinum on chest radiograph.
> Management principle: **reduce shear stress — heart rate first with a beta-blocker, then blood pressure with a vasodilator.** Giving a vasodilator first causes reflex tachycardia and increases shear. Urgent CT aortogram and cardiothoracic referral. `UNVERIFIED — target heart rate and blood pressure, and agent choice and dosing, require verification against eTG.`

> [!warning] ECG points that change management
> **ST depression in V1–V3** may be a **posterior STEMI** — obtain posterior leads (V7–V9) rather than treating it as NSTEMI.
> **Inferior STEMI** — obtain **right-sided leads (V4R)** for right ventricular infarction. **RV infarct is preload-dependent: nitrates cause profound hypotension and are contraindicated**, and these patients need fluid rather than vasodilatation. This is a high-yield and clinically important trap.
> **New left bundle branch block** in the right clinical context is treated as a STEMI equivalent, though "new" is often unknowable.
> **A single normal ECG does not exclude ACS.** Repeat it — during pain, and serially.

**S/Smx:** Character, onset speed, radiation, duration, exacerbating and relieving factors, and associated dyspnoea, diaphoresis, nausea, syncope or palpitations. Examine for blood pressure in both arms, murmurs, JVP, breath sounds, tracheal position, calf swelling, surgical emphysema and chest wall tenderness.

**Ix:** **ECG within ten minutes of arrival**, and repeated serially and during pain (*why:* it identifies the STEMI that requires immediate reperfusion, and dynamic change across serial tracings is more informative than any single one; *what:* ST elevation or depression, T wave change, new bundle branch block, arrhythmia — `UNVERIFIED — the door-to-ECG time target and STEMI voltage criteria require verification`). **Serial high-sensitivity troponin** at intervals defined by the local pathway (*why:* the **change** between samples, not the absolute first value, distinguishes acute myocardial injury from chronic elevation — see 0.5; *what:* baseline value and delta). CXR (*why:* pneumothorax, widened mediastinum, consolidation, pulmonary oedema, and it is quick; *what:* mediastinal width, pneumothorax, free air under the diaphragm, effusion). Bedside echocardiography (*why:* regional wall motion abnormality, RV strain, pericardial effusion and aortic root dilatation — it addresses four of the six life threats at once; *what:* wall motion, RV size, effusion, valve). FBC, UEC, glucose, lipids (*why:* anaemia precipitates ischaemia, renal function affects both troponin interpretation and drug dosing; *what:* anaemia, creatinine). D-dimer or CTPA per pre-test probability (*why:* PE; *what:* filling defect). **CT aortogram where dissection is possible** (*why:* the diagnosis is time-critical and missed dissection treated as ACS is lethal; *what:* intimal flap, false lumen). Risk stratification with a validated accelerated diagnostic protocol (*why:* Australian emergency departments use structured pathways — HEART, EDACS or similar — to identify low-risk patients suitable for early discharge, and applying one is more defensible than gestalt; *what:* risk category — `UNVERIFIED — score components and thresholds, and which pathway your site uses`).

### 0.1.1 Mx – Immediate
Monitored bed, oxygen **only if hypoxaemic** — routine oxygen in normoxic ACS is not beneficial and may be harmful. Aspirin. Analgesia. GTN, **excluding right ventricular infarction, severe aortic stenosis, and recent phosphodiesterase inhibitor use.** IV access, bloods, serial ECGs. **STEMI → immediate reperfusion**, primary PCI where achievable within the time target, otherwise fibrinolysis with transfer — a live consideration for rural South Australian presentations. `UNVERIFIED — all antiplatelet and anticoagulant agents and doses, and the PCI and fibrinolysis time targets, are omitted; obtain from your health network's ACS pathway.`

### 0.1.2 Mx – Definitive
Reperfusion and antithrombotic therapy for ACS. Anticoagulation for PE. Urgent surgery for type A dissection. Decompression for tension pneumothorax. Cross-refer [[F0.3]] 0.4–0.5.

### 0.1.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
Secondary prevention after ACS — antiplatelet therapy, statin, ACE inhibitor or ARB, beta-blocker, cardiac rehabilitation, smoking cessation, and management of diabetes, blood pressure and lipids. **Cardiac rehabilitation referral is under-done and improves both mortality and quality of life.**

---

## 0.2 Chest Tightness and Chronic Chest Pain

**D:** Recurrent or persistent chest discomfort over weeks to months, the central question being whether it represents stable coronary disease.

> [!tip] Stable angina — the three defining features
> **1. Constricting discomfort** in the chest, neck, shoulders, jaw or arms.
> **2. Precipitated by physical exertion** (and often more easily in cold weather, after meals, or with emotion).
> **3. Relieved by rest or GTN within about five minutes.**
> All three = **typical angina.** Two = **atypical.** One or none = **non-anginal.** This simple classification drives pre-test probability and is worth using explicitly.
> Severity is graded by the **Canadian Cardiovascular Society class** according to the level of exertion that provokes it. `UNVERIFIED — class descriptors.`

> [!danger] Crescendo pattern means unstable angina, not stable angina
> Angina that is **new, occurring at rest, more frequent, more severe, or provoked by progressively less exertion** is an acute coronary syndrome and is managed as such — not booked for an outpatient stress test. The change in pattern is the diagnosis.

> [!warning] Non-cardiac causes of chronic chest pain are common and frequently coexist
> **Gastro-oesophageal reflux** — the commonest, often burning and postprandial, but genuinely indistinguishable from angina in some patients. **Oesophageal spasm** — can be severe and GTN-responsive, which is exactly why GTN response is not diagnostic. **Musculoskeletal** — costochondritis, Tietze syndrome, chest wall strain; reproducible on palpation, though this does not exclude coexisting cardiac disease. **Anxiety and panic disorder** — a diagnosis of exclusion in anyone with cardiac risk factors, and one that is applied too readily to young women. **Herpes zoster** before the rash appears. **Pulmonary hypertension** and **aortic stenosis**, both of which cause exertional chest tightness. **Malignancy** with chest wall or mediastinal involvement.

**A/P:** Fixed atherosclerotic stenosis limits coronary flow reserve → at rest, flow is adequate → on exertion, myocardial oxygen demand rises and the stenosis prevents a matching increase in supply → **demand ischaemia** → anginal pain, relieved when demand falls. This is the mechanism that distinguishes stable angina from ACS, where the problem is an acute supply interruption from plaque rupture and thrombus rather than a demand mismatch.

**S/Smx:** As above. Examine for anaemia, aortic stenosis, hypertension, xanthelasma, peripheral vascular disease and heart failure signs.

**Ix:** Resting ECG (*why:* baseline, and may show prior infarction, hypertrophy or arrhythmia — though a normal resting ECG is common in significant coronary disease and is not reassuring; *what:* Q waves, LVH, ischaemic change). **CT coronary angiography** (*why:* increasingly the first-line test in low-to-intermediate risk patients — it has excellent negative predictive value, and a normal CTCA effectively excludes obstructive coronary disease; *what:* stenosis, plaque burden, calcium score). Functional testing — exercise stress ECG, stress echocardiography or myocardial perfusion imaging (*why:* demonstrates inducible ischaemia and its extent, which informs revascularisation decisions where anatomy alone does not; *what:* ischaemic territory and burden). Invasive coronary angiography (*why:* definitive anatomy with the option of intervention in the same procedure, reserved for high-risk or symptomatically refractory patients; *what:* stenosis, suitability for PCI or bypass). Echocardiography (*why:* identifies aortic stenosis and impaired ventricular function, both of which cause exertional chest pain and change management entirely; *what:* valve gradients, ejection fraction). FBC, TFTs, lipids, HbA1c, UEC (*why:* anaemia and thyrotoxicosis precipitate angina, and the metabolic risk factors need identifying and treating regardless; *what:* haemoglobin, lipids, glycaemia). Trial of proton pump inhibitor where reflux is plausible (*why:* practical and often diagnostic, though a response does not exclude coexisting cardiac disease; *what:* symptomatic response).

### 0.2.1 Mx – Immediate
Not usually required unless the pattern is unstable, in which case manage as 0.1.

### 0.2.2 Mx – Definitive
Antianginal therapy — beta-blocker or calcium channel blocker first line, with long-acting nitrates and other agents added. **Secondary prevention regardless of whether revascularisation occurs**: antiplatelet, statin, and blood pressure and diabetes control. Revascularisation for symptoms refractory to medical therapy, or for prognostic indications based on anatomy. `UNVERIFIED — agents and doses from eTG Cardiovascular.`

### 0.2.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
Cardiac rehabilitation, exercise, smoking cessation, weight and diet, and a written plan for what to do if the pattern changes — **patients should know that a change in their usual angina means calling an ambulance, not waiting for the clinic.**

---

## 0.3 Pleuritic Chest Pain

**D:** Sharp chest pain worsened by inspiration, coughing or movement, arising from the parietal pleura, pericardium or chest wall.

**A/P:** The **visceral pleura has no pain fibres**; the **parietal pleura is richly innervated** by intercostal nerves and, over the diaphragm, by the phrenic nerve. Inflammation, irritation or stretching of parietal pleura therefore produces sharp, well-localised, respirophasic pain — and **diaphragmatic pleural irritation refers to the shoulder tip via C3–C5**, which is why a subphrenic collection or splenic injury presents with shoulder pain.

> [!warning] Pleuritic pain does not exclude a cardiac cause
> **Pericarditis and, importantly, pulmonary embolism** both produce pleuritic pain. PE is the diagnosis most often missed in this presentation, precisely because "sharp and pleuritic" is taken as reassurance. Apply a structured PE risk assessment rather than dismissing pleuritic pain as musculoskeletal.

> [!tip] Differential
> **PE · pneumonia · pneumothorax · pericarditis · pleurisy from viral or connective tissue disease · rib fracture · costochondritis · herpes zoster before the rash · malignancy with pleural involvement · subphrenic pathology** referring upward.

> [!info] Acute pericarditis — the discriminators from STEMI
> | | Pericarditis | STEMI |
> |---|---|---|
> | Pain | **Positional — worse supine, better sitting forward**, pleuritic | Not positional, pressure-like |
> | ST elevation | **Widespread, concave ("saddle-shaped")**, not fitting a territory | **Regional**, convex, in a coronary territory |
> | Reciprocal change | Absent (except aVR and V1) | **Present** |
> | **PR segment** | **PR depression, with PR elevation in aVR** | Normal |
> | Rub | Present in a proportion | Absent |
> PR segment change is the most specific ECG feature of pericarditis and is routinely overlooked.
> Treatment is **NSAID plus colchicine**, the colchicine reducing recurrence substantially. `UNVERIFIED — doses and duration from eTG.`
> **Consider myocarditis** where troponin is raised, ventricular function is impaired, or arrhythmia occurs — that changes the disease from a nuisance to a potentially serious one, with implications including exercise restriction.

**S/Smx:** Sharp, localised, worse with deep breath, cough or movement, often with shallow guarded breathing. Look for a pleural or pericardial rub, focal chest signs, calf swelling, a vesicular rash in a dermatomal distribution, and chest wall tenderness.

**Ix:** ECG (*why:* distinguishes pericarditis from STEMI and may show right heart strain in PE; *what:* widespread concave ST elevation with PR depression, regional ST elevation, S1Q3T3 pattern). CXR (*why:* pneumothorax, consolidation, effusion, rib fracture; *what:* findings). Structured PE risk assessment with D-dimer or CTPA as indicated (*why:* PE is the missable diagnosis in this presentation and the assessment should be deliberate rather than intuitive; *what:* risk category, filling defect). Troponin (*why:* raised in myocarditis and in PE from RV strain, and its elevation in apparent pericarditis reclassifies the illness as myopericarditis; *what:* elevation). Echocardiography (*why:* pericardial effusion, tamponade physiology, ventricular function in myocarditis; *what:* effusion, wall motion). Inflammatory markers (*why:* support pericarditis and track response; *what:* raised CRP). Autoimmune screen where recurrent or with systemic features (*why:* connective tissue disease presents with serositis; *what:* ANA, ENA). Bedside lung ultrasound (*why:* rapid identification of pneumothorax, effusion and consolidation without moving the patient; *what:* absent lung sliding, effusion, B-lines).

### 0.3.1 Mx – Immediate
Analgesia, oxygen if hypoxaemic, and exclusion of PE and pneumothorax before attributing pain to the chest wall.

### 0.3.2 Mx – Definitive
Cause-specific. NSAID and colchicine for pericarditis. Anticoagulation for PE. Drainage for pneumothorax or empyema.

### 0.3.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
Follow-up echocardiography for pericarditis with effusion. **Exercise restriction in myocarditis** for a defined period, since exertion during active myocarditis is associated with arrhythmic death — this is important advice in young athletic patients and is frequently not given. `UNVERIFIED — duration of restriction.`

---

## 0.4 Coronary Vasospasm

**D:** Transient intense spasm of an epicardial coronary artery causing myocardial ischaemia, historically Prinzmetal or variant angina.

**A/P:** Hyperreactivity of coronary vascular smooth muscle, with endothelial dysfunction and autonomic influence → abrupt focal or diffuse spasm → **transient total or subtotal occlusion in an artery that may be angiographically normal** → transient ST elevation that resolves completely when the spasm relieves. Because the mechanism is smooth muscle constriction rather than thrombus, the treatment is vasodilatation rather than antithrombotic therapy.

> [!tip] The recognisable pattern
> **Pain at rest**, characteristically **at night or in the early hours of the morning**, in a patient who has preserved exercise tolerance — the opposite of the exertional pattern of fixed obstructive disease. **Transient ST elevation during pain that resolves entirely**, often with associated arrhythmia. Typically younger patients, **smoking being the dominant modifiable risk factor**, and more frequent in women than fixed atherosclerotic disease.

> [!warning] Triggers worth asking about
> **Smoking** · **cocaine and amphetamines** — a critical history in any young person with chest pain · triptans · **5-fluorouracil and capecitabine**, a well-recognised chemotherapy-induced coronary vasospasm that presents during infusion · cold exposure · hyperventilation · magnesium deficiency · alcohol withdrawal · **beta-blockers.**

> [!danger] Non-selective beta-blockers worsen vasospasm — this inverts standard ACS management
> Blocking β2-mediated coronary vasodilatation leaves **unopposed α-mediated vasoconstriction**, intensifying the spasm. **Calcium channel blockers are first-line**, with long-acting nitrates as add-on therapy.
> **The same principle applies to cocaine-associated chest pain**, where benzodiazepines and nitrates are the treatment and beta-blockers are traditionally avoided for the same unopposed-alpha reason. Cross-refer [[F0.1]] 0.4.

> [!info] MINOCA and INOCA
> **Myocardial infarction with non-obstructive coronary arteries** and **ischaemia with non-obstructive coronary arteries** are recognised entities. A patient with a genuine troponin rise and a normal angiogram has **not** had a false alarm — vasospasm, microvascular dysfunction, spontaneous coronary artery dissection, myocarditis and embolism all belong in that differential, and the workup continues rather than stopping. **Spontaneous coronary artery dissection specifically affects younger women, including peripartum**, and is frequently misattributed to anxiety.

**S/Smx:** Rest and nocturnal angina, sometimes severe, with preserved exertional capacity. Syncope or palpitations if arrhythmia accompanies the spasm.

**Ix:** **ECG during pain** (*why:* the diagnosis rests on demonstrating transient ischaemic change that resolves, and an ECG taken after the pain has settled is normal — so capturing one during an episode is the single most valuable investigation; *what:* transient ST elevation resolving completely). Ambulatory ECG monitoring (*why:* captures nocturnal and asymptomatic episodes that are otherwise never documented; *what:* transient ST shift). Troponin (*why:* may be raised if spasm is prolonged, distinguishing infarction from transient ischaemia; *what:* elevation). Coronary angiography (*why:* excludes fixed obstructive disease, which coexists in some patients, and may demonstrate spasm; *what:* normal or non-obstructive arteries). Provocative testing in specialist centres (*why:* confirms the diagnosis where it remains uncertain, though it carries risk and is not routine; *what:* induced spasm). **Urine toxicology for cocaine and amphetamines** (*why:* it changes the diagnosis, the treatment and the long-term advice, and the history is unreliable; *what:* stimulant use). Magnesium and electrolytes (*why:* deficiency contributes; *what:* low magnesium).

### 0.4.1 Mx – Immediate
Nitrates, which typically relieve the spasm rapidly. Calcium channel blocker. **Avoid beta-blockers.** Benzodiazepines where stimulant-related. Monitor for arrhythmia.

### 0.4.2 Mx – Definitive
Long-term calcium channel blocker, with long-acting nitrates added if needed. Cardiology involvement.

### 0.4.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
**Smoking cessation is the single most effective intervention** and should be framed as such. Stimulant cessation with drug and alcohol support. Avoidance of precipitating drugs, documented as a contraindication where chemotherapy-related.

---

## 0.5 Troponin and Cardiac Biomarkers

**D:** Cardiac troponin I and T are structural proteins released on myocyte injury, and the primary biomarkers of myocardial damage.

> [!danger] Troponin indicates myocardial injury — it does not indicate the cause of that injury
> **A raised troponin is not a diagnosis of myocardial infarction.** Infarction requires a **rise and/or fall** in troponin **plus clinical evidence of ischaemia** — symptoms, ECG changes, imaging evidence, or angiographic findings. Elevation without that context is **myocardial injury**, which has an entirely different differential and management.
> This distinction is the most consequential point in this section, and misunderstanding it leads to patients being anticoagulated, catheterised and labelled with coronary disease they do not have.

> [!tip] Type 1 versus Type 2 myocardial infarction — the distinction that changes treatment
> **Type 1** — **atherosclerotic plaque rupture with thrombus.** The classical ACS. Treatment is antithrombotic therapy and revascularisation.
> **Type 2** — **supply-demand mismatch without plaque rupture.** Caused by sepsis, tachyarrhythmia, severe anaemia, hypotension, hypoxia, severe hypertension. **Treatment is the underlying cause** — antiplatelets and catheterisation do not help and the bleeding risk is real.
> A septic patient with a raised troponin usually has type 2 injury. Treating them as ACS is a common error with real harm.

> [!warning] Non-ACS causes of a raised troponin
> **Pulmonary embolism** (RV strain) · **heart failure** · **myocarditis** · **chronic kidney disease** — where troponin is frequently chronically elevated and **the patient's own baseline is what matters, not the population reference range** · sepsis · tachyarrhythmia · cardiac contusion · **stroke and subarachnoid haemorrhage** · takotsubo cardiomyopathy · cardioversion and ablation · strenuous endurance exercise · infiltrative cardiomyopathy.

> [!info] High-sensitivity assays and the delta
> High-sensitivity troponin detects far lower concentrations and therefore identifies far more patients with detectable levels — most of whom do not have ACS. **The serial change (the "delta") between the initial and repeat sample is what distinguishes acute injury from chronic elevation.** A high but flat troponin over serial samples suggests chronic injury; a rising or falling pattern suggests an acute event.
> Assays have **sex-specific 99th percentile thresholds**, since normal troponin concentrations differ between men and women, and using a single threshold under-diagnoses in women.
> `UNVERIFIED — the 99th percentile values, sex-specific cut-offs, sampling intervals and delta thresholds are all assay-specific and site-specific. Obtain them from your own laboratory and chest pain pathway; troponin I and troponin T values are not interchangeable and neither are values between manufacturers.`

> [!danger] Don't order a troponin without a reason
> A troponin sent "for completeness" in a patient with no plausible ischaemic presentation generates a result that must then be explained, frequently leading to admission, further testing, anticoagulation and an incorrect label. **Order it when the answer will change what you do.**

> [!tip] The other biomarkers
> **BNP / NT-proBNP** — released on ventricular wall stretch. Most useful for its **negative predictive value**: a normal level makes heart failure unlikely in an undifferentiated breathless patient. Raised in renal impairment, AF, PE, sepsis and age; **lowered in obesity**, which is a genuine pitfall.
> **D-dimer** — a fibrin degradation product. Useful only in **low pre-test probability** to exclude PE or DVT; raised in infection, malignancy, pregnancy, surgery, trauma and old age, so a positive result in a high-risk patient adds nothing. Age-adjusted thresholds improve specificity in the elderly.
> **CK-MB** — largely superseded by troponin; retains a limited role in detecting reinfarction because it clears faster.
> **Myoglobin** — obsolete for this purpose.

**Ix:** Serial troponin per the local pathway with attention to the delta (*why:* the change over time is the diagnostic information, and a single value at presentation frequently cannot distinguish acute from chronic; *what:* baseline and change). Comparison with any **previous troponin values** in the record (*why:* a patient with chronic kidney disease may have a documented stable baseline, which immediately reframes an "abnormal" result; *what:* prior levels). ECG in parallel (*why:* troponin without ECG correlation cannot classify the event; *what:* ischaemic change). Echocardiography (*why:* regional wall motion abnormality supports ischaemic injury, global impairment suggests myocarditis, and RV strain suggests PE; *what:* wall motion pattern). UEC and eGFR (*why:* renal impairment raises baseline troponin and alters interpretation; *what:* creatinine, eGFR). Assessment for a type 2 precipitant — sepsis screen, haemoglobin, rhythm, blood pressure, oxygenation (*why:* identifying and treating the precipitant is the management of type 2 injury; *what:* infection, anaemia, arrhythmia, hypotension, hypoxia).

### 0.5.1 Mx – Immediate
Interpret in clinical context. Classify: STEMI, NSTEMI, type 2 injury, or non-ischaemic myocardial injury. Treat accordingly — **and treat the precipitant rather than the number in type 2.**

### 0.5.2 Mx – Definitive
As per the classification. Cardiology involvement for type 1 events and for unexplained significant elevation.

### 0.5.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
**Communicate the classification clearly in the discharge summary.** A patient discharged with "troponin rise" and no explanation acquires a coronary artery disease label that follows them for life, affects insurance and future care, and may lead to unnecessary long-term antiplatelet therapy. State explicitly whether this was a type 1 infarct, a type 2 injury, or a non-ischaemic elevation, and what the follow-up plan is.

---

> [!note] Cross-references
> Cardiogenic shock and acute pulmonary oedema → [[F0.3]] 0.3 and [[F0.5]] 0.3 · Massive PE and tamponade → [[F0.3]] 0.4–0.5 · Acute dyspnoea framework → [[A4]] 0.1 · Pneumothorax and pleuritic causes → [[A3]], [[F0.5]] · Cocaine and stimulant toxicity → [[F0.1]] 0.4 · Hypertensive emergency → [[B2]] · Arrhythmia → [[B3]] · Syncope → [[B4]] · Dyspepsia and reflux as a differential → [[C6]]


<!-- ===== SOURCE: B2_Hypertension_Spectrum.md ===== -->


> [!warning] Sourcing
> Written from model knowledge, not retrieved from guidelines. Mechanism, classification and management principles are reliable at intern level. **Every blood pressure threshold, target, reduction rate, dose and risk category carries an `UNVERIFIED` marker naming what to check, or has been omitted with the omission stated in place.** Verify against the **Heart Foundation Guideline for the diagnosis and management of hypertension in adults**, the **Australian CVD risk calculator** (revised in recent years — the older Framingham-based tool has been superseded), eTG Cardiovascular, and for pregnancy the **SOMANZ hypertension in pregnancy guideline**.

---

## 0.1 Elevated Blood Pressure

**D:** Persistently raised arterial blood pressure, diagnosed on repeated measurements or on ambulatory or home monitoring rather than on a single clinic reading.

> [!danger] Most "hypertension" is measured badly
> Measurement error is large enough to change management. The common faults, each of which can shift a reading substantially:
> **· Cuff too small** — the commonest error, and it **overestimates**. Cuff too large underestimates.
> **· Arm unsupported or below heart level** — overestimates. **· Back or feet unsupported** — overestimates.
> **· Talking during measurement**, by patient or clinician — overestimates.
> **· Legs crossed · full bladder · caffeine, smoking or exercise beforehand · inadequate rest period.**
> **A diagnosis of hypertension should never rest on one reading in one arm on one visit.** `UNVERIFIED — required rest period, number of readings and the interval between visits required for diagnosis.`

> [!tip] Measure both arms at least once
> A **persistent inter-arm difference** suggests subclavian stenosis, coarctation or aortic dissection, and once identified, the **arm with the higher reading** is the one used thereafter.

> [!info] White coat and masked hypertension
> **White coat hypertension** — raised in clinic, normal outside. Not entirely benign, but does not warrant the same treatment.
> **Masked hypertension** — normal in clinic, raised outside. **This is the dangerous one**, because it carries the cardiovascular risk of hypertension and goes untreated.
> **Ambulatory (ABPM) or home (HBPM) monitoring is the diagnostic standard**, and it is what distinguishes these. Out-of-office thresholds are **lower** than clinic thresholds — using clinic numbers to interpret home readings over-diagnoses. `UNVERIFIED — clinic, ABPM daytime, ABPM 24-hour and HBPM diagnostic thresholds all differ and require verification.`

> [!warning] Australia treats on absolute cardiovascular risk, not on blood pressure alone
> The Australian approach calculates **absolute 5-year cardiovascular disease risk** — incorporating age, sex, smoking, lipids, diabetes, blood pressure and other factors — and uses that to guide treatment. **A modestly raised blood pressure in a high-risk patient warrants treatment where the same reading in a low-risk young person may not.**
> The calculator was revised in recent years and the current Australian tool has different inputs and outputs from the older Framingham-derived version. It also includes **specific considerations for Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander adults**, who are recommended for risk assessment from a younger age. `UNVERIFIED — the current calculator, its risk category thresholds, the age at which assessment begins for different populations, and the treatment thresholds for each category all require verification.`

**A/P:** Blood pressure is the product of cardiac output and systemic vascular resistance. Essential hypertension involves a shift in the renal pressure-natriuresis relationship, increased sympathetic tone, renin-angiotensin-aldosterone activation, and arterial stiffening with age. Sustained elevation causes **arteriolar hyalinosis and hypertrophy**, accelerated atherosclerosis, left ventricular hypertrophy, and endothelial injury — producing the end-organ damage in brain, heart, kidney, retina and large vessels that is the actual reason for treating.

**S/Smx:** Almost always asymptomatic — which is the whole difficulty. Headache attributed to hypertension is usually coincidental at ordinary levels. Symptoms suggest either very severe elevation or a secondary cause.

**Ix:** Confirmatory ABPM or HBPM (*why:* distinguishes true, white coat and masked hypertension, and out-of-office readings predict outcomes better than clinic readings; *what:* average out-of-office pressure). UEC with eGFR and urine albumin-creatinine ratio (*why:* the kidney is both a cause and a target of hypertension, and albuminuria is an early marker of end-organ damage and a strong cardiovascular risk marker; *what:* creatinine, eGFR, albuminuria). Fasting lipids and HbA1c or fasting glucose (*why:* required to calculate absolute cardiovascular risk, which drives the treatment decision; *what:* lipid profile, glycaemia). ECG (*why:* left ventricular hypertrophy indicates established end-organ damage and raises risk; *what:* LVH, prior infarction, arrhythmia). FBC (*why:* anaemia and polycythaemia; *what:* haemoglobin). TFTs (*why:* thyroid disease affects blood pressure and is easily excluded; *what:* TSH). Fundoscopy (*why:* hypertensive retinopathy grades end-organ damage and, at the severe end, defines a hypertensive emergency; *what:* arteriolar narrowing, AV nipping, haemorrhages, exudates, papilloedema). Echocardiography where LVH is suspected or symptoms suggest heart failure (*why:* quantifies hypertrophy and diastolic dysfunction; *what:* wall thickness, diastolic parameters). Screening for secondary causes where indicated — see 0.4 (*why:* secondary hypertension is more common than historically appreciated and often specifically treatable; *what:* aldosterone-renin ratio, metanephrines, imaging).

### 0.1.1 Mx – Immediate
Not usually required. Severe asymptomatic elevation is managed as 0.2, **not** as an emergency.

### 0.1.2 Mx – Definitive
**Lifestyle intervention for everyone**, and it is genuinely effective: sodium reduction, weight loss, regular physical activity, alcohol reduction, smoking cessation, and dietary pattern change. Pharmacotherapy guided by absolute risk and by comorbidity — ACE inhibitors or ARBs, calcium channel blockers, and thiazide-like diuretics are the mainstays, with agent choice influenced by diabetes, heart failure, chronic kidney disease, gout, ethnicity and pregnancy potential.
**Do not combine an ACE inhibitor with an ARB** — the combination increases renal impairment, hyperkalaemia and hypotension without cardiovascular benefit.
**In pregnancy or planned pregnancy, ACE inhibitors and ARBs are contraindicated** and the agents used are different. Cross-refer [[O2]]. `UNVERIFIED — all agents, doses, combinations and blood pressure targets from eTG Cardiovascular and the Heart Foundation guideline.`

### 0.1.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
Adherence support — **non-adherence is the leading cause of apparently resistant hypertension**, and it is more usefully explored non-judgementally than assumed absent. Single-pill combinations improve adherence. Home monitoring engages patients. Regular review of renal function and electrolytes. Cross-refer [[P1]] Preventive Health.

---

## 0.2 Hypertensive Urgency

**D:** Severely elevated blood pressure **without acute end-organ damage.**

> [!danger] The defining principle: do not lower it quickly
> **Asymptomatic severe hypertension without end-organ damage does not require intravenous therapy, does not require rapid reduction, and in most cases does not require admission.**
> Rapid lowering is actively harmful. Chronic hypertension **shifts the cerebral autoregulation curve to the right** — the brain has adapted to perfusing at a higher pressure, and its lower autoregulatory limit has risen with it. **Dropping the pressure abruptly to "normal" therefore drops it below that shifted limit and causes cerebral hypoperfusion**, with watershed infarction, blindness from optic nerve ischaemia, myocardial ischaemia and acute kidney injury. The same physiology explains why the target is gradual.
> Treating the number rather than the patient is one of the most common avoidable harms in this area.

> [!tip] Look for why it is high today
> **Non-adherence or running out of medication** — by far the commonest reason, and worth asking about directly and without judgement.
> **Pain · anxiety · urinary retention** — all raise blood pressure substantially and all resolve with treatment of the cause. **Check for a full bladder**; it is a genuinely common and instantly reversible cause of alarming readings in inpatients.
> **Drugs** — NSAIDs, decongestants, stimulants, cocaine, venlafaxine, corticosteroids, oral contraceptives, liquorice.
> **Withdrawal** — abrupt cessation of clonidine or a beta-blocker causes rebound hypertension; alcohol withdrawal likewise.
> **White coat effect in a distressed patient.**

**S/Smx:** By definition, none attributable to end-organ damage. The clinical task is a careful search for the features that would reclassify this as an emergency — see 0.3.

**Ix:** Repeat measurement after a period of rest, with correct technique (*why:* a substantial proportion of alarming readings fall considerably on repeat once the patient has sat quietly, and acting on the first reading leads to over-treatment; *what:* repeat value). Targeted assessment for end-organ damage — neurological examination, **fundoscopy**, cardiovascular examination, ECG, UEC and urinalysis (*why:* this is the assessment that separates urgency from emergency and therefore determines the entire management pathway; *what:* focal deficit, retinal haemorrhage or papilloedema, pulmonary oedema, ischaemic ECG change, rising creatinine, proteinuria or haematuria). Medication history including adherence and recent cessation (*why:* identifies the reversible cause in most cases; *what:* missed doses, withdrawal, new interacting drug). Bladder scan in an inpatient (*why:* retention is common, painful and instantly reversible; *what:* retained volume). Pregnancy test in a woman of reproductive age (*why:* pre-eclampsia is managed entirely differently and is a genuine emergency; *what:* positive test).

### 0.2.1 Mx – Immediate
Rest, analgesia, relieve retention, address anxiety, and **recheck.** Restart or adjust oral antihypertensive therapy. **Avoid short-acting nifedipine capsules** and other agents causing abrupt uncontrolled falls.

### 0.2.2 Mx – Definitive
Oral therapy with **gradual reduction over days to weeks**, not hours. Outpatient follow-up arranged before discharge, with a clear review interval.

### 0.2.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
Adherence support, secondary cause screening if indicated, and cardiovascular risk management. Ensure the patient leaves with a prescription they can actually fill and a follow-up appointment they can actually attend — cost and access are frequent contributors.

---

## 0.3 Hypertensive Emergency

**D:** Severe hypertension **with acute, ongoing end-organ damage.** The organ damage defines the emergency; the number alone does not.

> [!tip] The syndromes, and how they differ in management
> | Syndrome | Features | Management nuance |
> |---|---|---|
> | **Hypertensive encephalopathy** | Headache, confusion, visual disturbance, seizures, papilloedema; posterior changes on MRI (PRES) | Controlled gradual reduction |
> | **Acute ischaemic stroke** | Focal deficit | **Permissive hypertension** — lowering worsens penumbral perfusion, *unless* thrombolysis is planned, when a lower threshold applies |
> | **Intracerebral haemorrhage** | Focal deficit, headache, reduced GCS | Earlier and more active lowering than in ischaemic stroke |
> | **Acute pulmonary oedema (SCAPE)** | Severe dyspnoea, hypertensive, distressed | **Nitrates and NIV**, not primarily diuresis — cross-refer [[F0.5]] 0.3 |
> | **Acute coronary syndrome** | Chest pain, ECG change | Nitrates, beta-blockade |
> | **Aortic dissection** | Tearing pain, BP differential | **The exception — rapid, aggressive reduction. Rate control first, then vasodilator** |
> | **Acute kidney injury / malignant hypertension** | Rising creatinine, haematuria, proteinuria, retinopathy | Controlled reduction; renal function may worsen transiently |
> | **Pre-eclampsia / eclampsia** | Pregnancy, proteinuria, hyperreflexia, seizures | **Magnesium sulfate and delivery** — cross-refer [[O2]] |
> | **Phaeochromocytoma crisis / sympathomimetic** | Paroxysmal, diaphoresis, palpitations | **Alpha blockade before beta blockade** — see 0.4 |

> [!danger] Fundoscopy is the examination that most often makes the diagnosis
> **Malignant (accelerated) hypertension** is defined by severe hypertension with **retinal haemorrhages, cotton wool spots, hard exudates or papilloedema.** It is the finding that converts an alarming number into an emergency, it is available at the bedside for free, and it is the assessment most often skipped. Look at the fundi in any patient with severe hypertension.

> [!warning] The rate of reduction is as important as the target
> Outside aortic dissection, the principle is a **controlled, partial reduction over the first hour, then a further gradual reduction over the following day**, using a **titratable intravenous agent in a monitored setting** so that an overshoot can be corrected within minutes. `UNVERIFIED — the percentage reduction targets for the first hour and the subsequent 24 hours, the specific targets in aortic dissection and in stroke with and without thrombolysis, and all agent choices and doses are omitted. Obtain from eTG and your health network's protocol.`
> Agents used include labetalol, glyceryl trinitrate, hydralazine, sodium nitroprusside and clevidipine. **Sodium nitroprusside accumulates as thiocyanate and cyanide with prolonged use or renal impairment**, which limits its duration of use and mandates monitoring.

> [!danger] Malignant hypertension can cause a microangiopathic haemolytic anaemia
> Severe hypertension damages endothelium and shears red cells, producing **anaemia with schistocytes and thrombocytopenia** — a picture that mimics thrombotic thrombocytopenic purpura or haemolytic uraemic syndrome. Recognising hypertension as the cause avoids plasma exchange for a condition that responds to blood pressure control. Cross-refer [[J1]].

**S/Smx:** Determined by the organ involved — headache, confusion, seizures, visual loss, focal deficit, chest pain, dyspnoea, oliguria, or the features of pre-eclampsia.

**Ix:** **Fundoscopy** (*why:* as above — grades retinopathy and may be the finding that establishes the emergency; *what:* haemorrhages, exudates, papilloedema). ECG and troponin (*why:* acute coronary syndrome and LV strain; *what:* ischaemic change, troponin rise). CXR (*why:* pulmonary oedema and widened mediastinum; *what:* oedema, mediastinal width). UEC with eGFR, and urinalysis with microscopy (*why:* acute kidney injury and an active urinary sediment establish renal involvement; *what:* rising creatinine, proteinuria, haematuria, red cell casts). **FBC with blood film and LDH** (*why:* identifies the microangiopathic haemolysis above; *what:* schistocytes, thrombocytopenia, raised LDH). CT or MRI brain (*why:* distinguishes ischaemic stroke, haemorrhage and posterior reversible encephalopathy syndrome, each of which has a different blood pressure target; *what:* infarct, haemorrhage, posterior white matter change). CT aortogram where dissection is suspected (*why:* it is the exception to every other management rule here; *what:* intimal flap). Pregnancy test and, if positive, pre-eclampsia workup (*why:* entirely different management; *what:* proteinuria, LFTs, platelets, urate). Urinary or plasma metanephrines where paroxysmal features (*why:* phaeochromocytoma crisis requires alpha blockade first; *what:* raised metanephrines). Toxicology (*why:* stimulant-induced hypertensive crisis is managed with benzodiazepines rather than beta-blockade; *what:* cocaine, amphetamines).

### 0.3.1 Mx – Immediate
Monitored setting with arterial line where feasible. **Identify the syndrome first**, because it determines the target and the agent. Titratable IV therapy with controlled reduction. Treat the specific condition — magnesium and delivery in eclampsia, nitrates and NIV in SCAPE, rate then pressure control in dissection, permissive hypertension in ischaemic stroke.

### 0.3.2 Mx – Definitive
Cause-specific, with ICU or HDU involvement, transition to oral therapy once stable, and specialty referral.

### 0.3.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
**Screen for a secondary cause** — hypertensive emergency is considerably more likely than routine hypertension to have one. Establish long-term control with follow-up, and address the adherence or access problem that frequently underlies it.

---

## 0.4 Paroxysmal Hypertension and Secondary Causes

**D:** Episodic or sustained hypertension caused by an identifiable underlying condition rather than essential hypertension.

> [!tip] When to suspect a secondary cause
> **· Onset before roughly age 30, or new onset after 60** · **severe or resistant hypertension** — uncontrolled despite three agents including a diuretic · **sudden worsening** in previously stable hypertension · **hypokalaemia**, spontaneous or diuretic-provoked · end-organ damage disproportionate to the recorded pressure · **paroxysmal symptoms** · a hypertensive emergency · an abdominal bruit · features of an endocrine syndrome.
> `UNVERIFIED — the definition of resistant hypertension and the age thresholds require verification.`

> [!warning] Primary aldosteronism is far more common than traditionally taught
> Historically considered rare, it is now recognised in a substantial minority of hypertensive patients, and a larger proportion of resistant hypertension. **Hypokalaemia is present in only a minority — its absence does not exclude the diagnosis**, which is the single most important updated point here.
> Screening is by the **aldosterone-to-renin ratio**, and interpretation is complicated because many antihypertensives interfere with it — **beta-blockers, ACE inhibitors, ARBs, diuretics and particularly mineralocorticoid receptor antagonists** all affect the result, so the drugs a hypertensive patient is already on are exactly the ones that confound the test. `UNVERIFIED — which drugs must be withheld and for how long before testing, and the ratio thresholds, require verification and specialist input.`
> It matters because it is **specifically treatable** — surgically curable if unilateral, and highly responsive to mineralocorticoid receptor antagonism if bilateral — and because it carries excess cardiovascular risk beyond that of the blood pressure alone.

> [!danger] Phaeochromocytoma — alpha blockade before beta blockade
> **The classic triad is episodic headache, palpitations and diaphoresis**, often with pallor rather than flushing, anxiety and a sense of doom, occurring in paroxysms against a background of sustained or labile hypertension.
> **Giving a beta-blocker first is dangerous.** Blocking β2-mediated vasodilatation leaves catecholamine-driven **unopposed alpha vasoconstriction**, precipitating a hypertensive crisis. **Alpha blockade is established first, and beta blockade is added afterwards** for reflex tachycardia. This is the same unopposed-alpha principle that appears in cocaine toxicity and coronary vasospasm, and it is examined repeatedly.
> Screening is by **plasma free metanephrines or 24-hour urinary metanephrines**, which are more sensitive than catecholamines. False positives are common with tricyclics, levodopa, stimulants, and physiological stress, so pre-test conditions matter. Associated with familial syndromes — **MEN2, von Hippel-Lindau, neurofibromatosis type 1, SDH mutations** — so a positive result warrants genetic consideration and family screening.

> [!tip] The other secondary causes worth knowing
> **Renal parenchymal disease** — the commonest secondary cause overall. Chronic kidney disease of any aetiology.
> **Renovascular** — **atherosclerotic** in older patients with vascular disease, and **fibromuscular dysplasia** in younger women, classically with a "string of beads" appearance on angiography. Suspect with an abdominal bruit, or **a significant rise in creatinine after starting an ACE inhibitor or ARB**, which suggests bilateral disease.
> **Obstructive sleep apnoea** — very common, under-diagnosed, and a major contributor to resistant hypertension.
> **Cushing syndrome** — including **exogenous corticosteroids**, which are the commonest cause and are on the medication list.
> **Coarctation of the aorta** — upper limb hypertension with **radiofemoral delay** and weak femoral pulses. Classically diagnosed in young adults after years of "essential" hypertension because nobody felt the femoral pulses. **Feel the femorals in any young hypertensive patient.**
> **Thyroid disease** — both directions. **Hyperparathyroidism.** **Acromegaly.**
> **Drugs** — NSAIDs, combined oral contraceptives, venlafaxine and other SNRIs, corticosteroids, decongestants, stimulants, ciclosporin, erythropoietin, and **liquorice**, which causes an apparent mineralocorticoid excess.

**S/Smx:** Paroxysmal headache, palpitations, sweating and pallor in phaeochromocytoma. Muscle weakness, cramps and polyuria in aldosteronism with hypokalaemia. Snoring and daytime somnolence in OSA. Cushingoid features. Otherwise asymptomatic.

**Ix:** **Plasma free metanephrines or 24-hour urinary metanephrines** (*why:* the sensitive screening test for phaeochromocytoma, which must be excluded before any surgical or invasive management and before beta blockade; *what:* markedly raised metanephrines). **Aldosterone-to-renin ratio** with attention to interfering medications (*why:* screens for primary aldosteronism, which is common, treatable and frequently missed; *what:* raised ratio requiring confirmatory testing). UEC with potassium, and acid-base (*why:* hypokalaemic metabolic alkalosis supports mineralocorticoid excess, though its absence does not exclude it; *what:* low potassium, alkalosis). Renal ultrasound with Doppler, or CT/MR angiography (*why:* identifies renal parenchymal disease, size asymmetry and renal artery stenosis; *what:* small kidney, stenosis, fibromuscular changes). Overnight dexamethasone suppression test or 24-hour urinary free cortisol (*why:* screens for Cushing syndrome; *what:* failure of suppression). TFTs and calcium (*why:* thyroid and parathyroid disease; *what:* TSH, calcium, PTH). Sleep study (*why:* OSA is a common and treatable contributor to resistant hypertension; *what:* apnoea-hypopnoea index). Echocardiography and **femoral pulse examination**, with CT or MR aortography if coarctation is suspected (*why:* coarctation is curable and is diagnosed by an examination finding that costs nothing; *what:* radiofemoral delay, aortic narrowing). Adrenal imaging **only after biochemical confirmation** (*why:* adrenal incidentalomas are common and imaging first leads to investigating a non-functioning nodule while missing the real cause; *what:* adenoma, hyperplasia, mass).

### 0.4.1 Mx – Immediate
For a phaeochromocytoma crisis: **alpha blockade first**, then beta blockade, in a monitored setting. For stimulant-induced crisis: benzodiazepines and vasodilators, avoiding beta-blockade.

### 0.4.2 Mx – Definitive
Cause-specific and often curative — adrenalectomy after adequate alpha blockade for phaeochromocytoma, adrenalectomy or mineralocorticoid receptor antagonist for primary aldosteronism, revascularisation or medical therapy for renovascular disease, CPAP for OSA, repair of coarctation, cessation of the causative drug. Endocrinology and hypertension specialist involvement.

### 0.4.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
**Genetic testing and family screening** for phaeochromocytoma and paraganglioma, given the substantial proportion with a heritable syndrome. Long-term surveillance for recurrence. Continued cardiovascular risk management, since the vascular damage accumulated before diagnosis does not reverse with cure of the cause.

---

> [!note] Cross-references
> Aortic dissection and the six chest pain life threats → [[B1]] 0.1 · Acute pulmonary oedema and SCAPE → [[F0.5]] 0.3 · Stroke and focal deficit → [[D3]] · Pre-eclampsia and eclampsia → [[O2]] · Sympathomimetic toxicity and unopposed alpha → [[F0.1]] 0.4 · Coronary vasospasm → [[B1]] 0.4 · Microangiopathic haemolysis → [[J1]] · Obstructive sleep apnoea → [[13_05b_ENT_-_Stridor__Croup__Epiglottitis__Laryngomalacia__OSA]] · Preventive health and absolute risk → [[P1]]


<!-- ===== SOURCE: B3_Arrhythmia__Bradycardia_and_Cardiac_Devices.md ===== -->


> [!warning] Sourcing
> Written from model knowledge, not retrieved from guidelines. ECG reasoning, classification and discriminators are reliable at intern level. **Every dose, energy level, rate threshold, score cut-off and time window carries an `UNVERIFIED` marker naming what to check, or has been omitted with the omission stated in place.** Verify against **ANZCOR Guideline 11.9 (peri-arrest arrhythmias)**, current **CSANZ / National Heart Foundation atrial fibrillation guidance**, and eTG Cardiovascular. Cardioversion energies and antiarrhythmic dosing are omitted throughout.

---

## 0.1 Tachyarrhythmia — The Approach

**D:** A heart rate above the normal range arising from an abnormal rhythm rather than an appropriate physiological response.

> [!danger] The first question is always stability, not rhythm
> **Adverse features — shock, syncope, myocardial ischaemia, or heart failure — mean the patient needs synchronised DC cardioversion now**, regardless of what the rhythm turns out to be. Do not spend time on rhythm analysis in an unstable patient, and do not attempt drug therapy first.
> Conversely, in a **stable** patient there is time to obtain a 12-lead ECG, think, and get help. The commonest errors are cardioverting a stable patient in a hurry and drug-treating an unstable one slowly.
> `UNVERIFIED — synchronised cardioversion energies, sedation, and the number of attempts before escalation are omitted; obtain from ANZCOR Guideline 11.9.`

> [!tip] The four-box classification
> Once stable, two questions sort almost every tachycardia:
> | | **Regular** | **Irregular** |
> |---|---|---|
> | **Narrow (QRS normal)** | Sinus tachycardia · AVNRT · AVRT · atrial flutter with fixed block · atrial tachycardia | **AF** · flutter with variable block · **multifocal atrial tachycardia** |
> | **Broad (QRS wide)** | **VT until proven otherwise** · SVT with aberrancy · antidromic AVRT | **Polymorphic VT / torsades** · **pre-excited AF** · AF with bundle branch block |
> **The irregular broad-complex box contains the two rhythms where the wrong drug kills**, which is why it is worth recognising as a box rather than as a list.

> [!warning] Is the tachycardia the problem, or the response to it?
> **Sinus tachycardia is almost never the primary problem.** It is the response to hypovolaemia, sepsis, pain, anxiety, hypoxia, anaemia, PE, thyrotoxicosis, alcohol withdrawal or a drug. **Rate-controlling a compensatory sinus tachycardia removes the compensation and causes decompensation.** Ask what the heart is compensating for before slowing it.
> The same caution applies to AF with a rapid ventricular response in sepsis — the sepsis frequently needs treating more than the rate does.

**S/Smx:** Palpitations, dyspnoea, chest pain, lightheadedness, syncope, anxiety, polyuria (from atrial natriuretic peptide release in SVT). Adverse features as above.

**Ix:** **12-lead ECG during the tachycardia, and a rhythm strip** (*why:* a rhythm captured only on the monitor is frequently uninterpretable afterwards, and the tachycardia may terminate before anyone thinks to record it — the 12-lead during the episode is the single most valuable investigation and is routinely missed; *what:* rate, regularity, QRS width, P wave relationship, axis). Post-conversion 12-lead ECG (*why:* reveals pre-excitation, prior infarction, prolonged QT and Brugada pattern, all of which are invisible during the tachycardia and all of which change management; *what:* delta wave, Q waves, QT interval). UEC with potassium, magnesium and calcium (*why:* electrolyte disturbance both causes and perpetuates arrhythmia and is correctable; *what:* low potassium and magnesium). TFTs (*why:* thyrotoxicosis is a common and reversible driver, particularly of AF; *what:* suppressed TSH). FBC (*why:* anaemia precipitates and sustains tachycardia; *what:* haemoglobin). Troponin (*why:* interpreted with care — tachycardia itself raises troponin through demand ischaemia (type 2), so an elevation does not establish ACS as the cause; *what:* elevation in context — cross-refer [[B1]] 0.5). Bedside echocardiography (*why:* structural heart disease strongly favours VT in a broad complex tachycardia, and identifies the cardiomyopathy that changes long-term management; *what:* LV function, wall motion, valvular disease). Drug and toxicology history (*why:* stimulants, QT-prolonging drugs, digoxin toxicity and caffeine are all correctable causes; *what:* offending agent).

### 0.1.1 Mx – Immediate
Assess stability. Unstable → synchronised cardioversion. Stable → 12-lead, correct electrolytes, identify the rhythm, treat accordingly, and get senior help.

### 0.1.2 Mx – Definitive
Rhythm-specific, as below.

### 0.1.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
Identify and treat precipitants. Cardiology referral for recurrent or symptomatic arrhythmia, with consideration of ablation.

---

## 0.2 Narrow Complex Tachycardia

**D:** Tachycardia with a QRS of normal width, therefore originating at or above the AV node.

**A/P:** A narrow QRS means the ventricles are being depolarised through the normal His-Purkinje system, so the arrhythmia's origin must be supraventricular. **AVNRT** uses a re-entry circuit within the AV node itself; **AVRT** uses an accessory pathway connecting atrium and ventricle; **atrial flutter** is a macro-re-entrant circuit within the atrium; **AF** is disorganised atrial activity.

> [!tip] Regular at about 150 — think flutter
> Atrial flutter has an atrial rate of roughly 300, and with **2:1 conduction the ventricular rate is about 150 and metronomically regular.** Any regular narrow tachycardia near 150 should prompt a deliberate hunt for flutter waves — they hide in the T waves and are easiest to see in the inferior leads and V1. **Adenosine, by transiently blocking the AV node, unmasks them**, which makes it a diagnostic as well as a therapeutic agent.

> [!info] Vagal manoeuvres — do them properly
> The **modified Valsalva** — standard strain, then immediately laid flat with the legs passively raised — is substantially more effective than the conventional Valsalva and should be the default technique. It is free, it works in a meaningful proportion of SVT, and it is worth doing well before reaching for a drug. `UNVERIFIED — strain pressure and duration.`

> [!warning] Adenesine — warn the patient first
> Adenosine causes a brief but genuinely unpleasant sensation of chest tightness, flushing and impending doom, and patients who are not warned find it frightening. Explain it, have the defibrillator and a rhythm strip running, and give it **rapidly through a large proximal vein with an immediate flush** — its half-life is measured in seconds and a slow push simply does not work.
> **Caution in asthma**, where it can cause bronchospasm. **Reduce the dose** in patients on dipyridamole or with a transplanted heart, where the effect is markedly potentiated. `UNVERIFIED — doses, dose escalation and the adjustments above.`

> [!danger] Do not give AV nodal blocking drugs in pre-excited AF
> An **irregular, broad, very fast** tachycardia in a patient with Wolff-Parkinson-White is atrial fibrillation conducting down an accessory pathway. **Adenosine, verapamil, diltiazem, beta-blockers and digoxin block the AV node and thereby push conduction preferentially down the accessory pathway**, accelerating the ventricular rate and precipitating ventricular fibrillation.
> **Treat with synchronised cardioversion, or with an antiarrhythmic that acts on the accessory pathway** under specialist direction. This is one of the highest-consequence drug errors in acute cardiology and it is examined frequently.

> [!info] Multifocal atrial tachycardia
> **At least three distinct P wave morphologies with varying PR intervals, irregularly irregular, at a rate above 100.** It is routinely misdiagnosed as AF — **the presence of discrete P waves is what distinguishes it**, and looking for them is the whole diagnosis.
> Strongly associated with **severe COPD and hypoxia**, and with theophylline toxicity, hypokalaemia and hypomagnesaemia.
> **The treatment is the underlying lung disease and the electrolytes, not the rhythm.** Antiarrhythmics work poorly, cardioversion does not work at all (it is not re-entrant), and **beta-blockers are relatively contraindicated in the severe airways disease these patients have.** Magnesium and verapamil are sometimes used. Recognising MAT primarily means recognising that the patient's respiratory disease is decompensated.

**S/Smx:** Abrupt onset and offset palpitations in AVNRT and AVRT — patients often describe it starting and stopping "like a switch", which distinguishes it from sinus tachycardia. Neck pulsation from cannon A waves. Dyspnoea, chest discomfort, presyncope.

**Ix:** As per 0.1, with particular attention to the **post-conversion ECG for a delta wave** (*why:* identifies Wolff-Parkinson-White, which changes acute management, long-term management and driving and sporting advice; *what:* short PR with slurred QRS upstroke). Continuous rhythm recording during vagal manoeuvres and adenosine (*why:* the response is diagnostic — termination suggests an AV-node-dependent circuit, transient slowing unmasking flutter waves suggests atrial flutter, and no effect suggests VT or an atrial tachycardia; *what:* the response). Ambulatory monitoring where episodes are paroxysmal and undocumented (*why:* the diagnosis depends on capturing the rhythm; *what:* the arrhythmia during symptoms). TFTs and electrolytes as above.

### 0.2.1 Mx – Immediate
Unstable → cardioversion. Stable → vagal manoeuvres, then adenosine, then rate-limiting calcium channel blocker or beta-blocker as directed — **except in pre-excited AF.** Correct electrolytes.

### 0.2.2 Mx – Definitive
**Catheter ablation is curative in AVNRT and AVRT** with high success rates, and should be offered rather than committing a young patient to lifelong drug therapy. Flutter ablation is likewise highly effective.

### 0.2.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
Trigger avoidance — caffeine, alcohol, stimulants, sleep deprivation. Teach the patient the modified Valsalva so they can terminate episodes themselves. Cardiology follow-up, and driving advice where syncope occurred — cross-refer [[A10]] 0.6.

---

## 0.3 Broad Complex Tachycardia

**D:** Tachycardia with a widened QRS, which must be treated as ventricular tachycardia until proven otherwise.

> [!danger] Assume VT
> **The great majority of broad complex tachycardias are VT**, and the proportion rises to near-certainty in a patient with known ischaemic heart disease or previous myocardial infarction. **Treating VT as SVT is dangerous; treating SVT as VT is usually harmless.** Where the diagnosis is uncertain, treat as VT.
> **Verapamil given to a patient with VT causes profound hypotension and cardiac arrest.** This is the specific error the "assume VT" rule exists to prevent.

> [!tip] Features favouring VT over SVT with aberrancy
> **· AV dissociation** — P waves marching through at their own rate, independent of the QRS. The most specific sign, and worth hunting for.
> **· Capture beats** (a normal narrow QRS interrupting the run) and **fusion beats** (a hybrid morphology) — both essentially diagnostic.
> **· Very broad QRS**, extreme axis deviation, **concordance** across the precordial leads (all positive or all negative).
> **· Known structural heart disease or prior MI** — clinically the single most useful discriminator.
> Formal algorithms exist (Brugada, Vereckei) but are not needed to make the safe decision. `UNVERIFIED — algorithm criteria.`

> [!warning] Polymorphic VT and torsades de pointes
> **Torsades** is polymorphic VT on a background of a **prolonged QT**, with the characteristic twisting-around-the-baseline morphology.
> Causes of QT prolongation: **drugs** — many antipsychotics, antiemetics including ondansetron and metoclopramide, macrolides, quinolones, antiarrhythmics, methadone, and combinations of these · **electrolytes** — hypokalaemia, hypomagnesaemia, hypocalcaemia · **congenital long QT syndromes** · bradycardia · hypothyroidism.
> **Management: intravenous magnesium regardless of the serum level, aggressive potassium and magnesium correction, and cessation of every QT-prolonging drug.** Increasing the heart rate shortens the QT, so overdrive pacing or isoprenaline is used in bradycardia-dependent torsades. **If pulseless, it is defibrillated.** `UNVERIFIED — magnesium dose and potassium targets.`
> **Check the medication chart** — polypharmacy with two or three QT-prolonging agents is a common iatrogenic cause and is entirely preventable.

**S/Smx:** Palpitations, chest pain, dyspnoea, presyncope, syncope, or cardiac arrest. Haemodynamic tolerance varies widely and **a patient who is talking to you can still be in VT** — stability does not exclude it.

**Ix:** 12-lead ECG during the tachycardia, examined specifically for AV dissociation, capture and fusion beats (*why:* these findings settle the diagnosis and are only visible on a 12-lead, not on a monitor strip; *what:* dissociated P waves, capture and fusion complexes). Post-conversion ECG (*why:* prior infarction, prolonged QT, Brugada pattern and epsilon waves of arrhythmogenic right ventricular cardiomyopathy all appear only in sinus rhythm; *what:* Q waves, QT, Brugada morphology). **Potassium, magnesium and calcium** (*why:* correctable and frequently causative, and magnesium is given in torsades regardless; *what:* low values). Troponin (*why:* ischaemia as a cause, interpreted with the demand-ischaemia caveat; *what:* elevation). Echocardiography (*why:* structural disease supports VT and identifies the cardiomyopathy driving it; *what:* impaired LV, scar, RV abnormality). Full medication review (*why:* QT-prolonging drug combinations are a common preventable cause; *what:* offending agents). Digoxin level where relevant (*why:* toxicity causes a range of arrhythmias and is treatable; *what:* level with potassium).

### 0.3.1 Mx – Immediate
Unstable → **synchronised cardioversion.** Pulseless → **defibrillate**, cross-refer [[A1]] 0.4. Stable VT → antiarrhythmic under senior direction, with correction of electrolytes. Torsades → magnesium. **Do not give verapamil.**

### 0.3.2 Mx – Definitive
Treat ischaemia. Cardiology involvement. **Implantable cardioverter defibrillator** assessment for VT in the context of structural heart disease. Ablation for recurrent monomorphic VT.

### 0.3.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
**Family screening** where a channelopathy or inherited cardiomyopathy is identified — long QT syndrome, Brugada syndrome, ARVC and hypertrophic cardiomyopathy all have heritable forms and the index case's relatives may be at risk of sudden death. Driving restriction and documentation of QT-prolonging drug avoidance.

---

## 0.4 Atrial Fibrillation and Flutter

**D:** Disorganised atrial electrical activity with an irregularly irregular ventricular response (AF), or organised macro-re-entrant atrial activity at around 300 per minute with variable AV conduction (flutter).

**A/P:** Atrial remodelling and ectopic activity, frequently originating around the **pulmonary vein ostia**, produce chaotic atrial depolarisation → loss of coordinated atrial contraction → **loss of the atrial contribution to ventricular filling**, which matters most in diastolic dysfunction and stenotic valve disease → plus **stasis in the left atrial appendage** → thrombus formation → embolic stroke. The stroke risk is the reason AF matters more than the palpitations suggest.

> [!danger] Anticoagulation is decided independently of everything else about the rhythm
> **The decision to anticoagulate depends on stroke risk, not on whether the AF is paroxysmal, persistent or permanent, and not on whether a rhythm control strategy has succeeded.**
> A patient in sinus rhythm after cardioversion or ablation still has the stroke risk their risk score describes. Stopping anticoagulation because "the AF is fixed" is a recognised and serious error.
> Risk is stratified with **CHA₂DS₂-VASc**, and bleeding risk considered with tools such as HAS-BLED — **which is used to identify and modify reversible bleeding risk factors, not to withhold anticoagulation.** `UNVERIFIED — score components, point allocations and the thresholds at which anticoagulation is recommended require verification against current CSANZ/Heart Foundation guidance.`

> [!warning] Which anticoagulant
> **Direct oral anticoagulants are preferred to warfarin** in most patients — comparable or better efficacy, less intracranial haemorrhage, no monitoring.
> **The exceptions requiring warfarin are mechanical prosthetic heart valves and moderate-to-severe mitral stenosis**, historically termed "valvular AF". DOACs are contraindicated in these.
> Dosing is adjusted for **renal function, age and weight**, and under-dosing a DOAC to reduce bleeding risk is common and leaves the patient with the bleeding risk and without the stroke protection. **Aspirin is not adequate stroke prevention in AF** and should not be used as a substitute.

> [!tip] Rate versus rhythm control
> Both are reasonable initial strategies for most patients, and **rate control does not mean giving up.** Rhythm control is favoured in younger patients, those with symptoms despite rate control, heart failure, and **early after first diagnosis**, where there is evidence that early rhythm control improves outcomes.
> Rate control agents: beta-blockers and non-dihydropyridine calcium channel blockers, with digoxin as an adjunct — **digoxin controls rate at rest but poorly on exertion**, so it is a poor sole agent in an active patient.
> `UNVERIFIED — agent choice, doses and rate targets from eTG.`

> [!danger] The 48-hour rule for cardioversion
> Cardioverting AF of uncertain or greater than 48 hours' duration without either **adequate anticoagulation for a defined period beforehand** or a **transoesophageal echocardiogram excluding atrial thrombus** risks embolising a thrombus at the moment sinus rhythm and atrial contraction return.
