> **Anticoagulation is also required for a period after cardioversion**, because atrial mechanical function ("atrial stunning") recovers more slowly than electrical function and the stasis persists. `UNVERIFIED — the duration cut-off, the pre- and post-cardioversion anticoagulation periods.`

> [!warning] Always look for the precipitant in new AF
> **Sepsis · thyrotoxicosis · alcohol binge ("holiday heart") · pulmonary embolism · electrolyte disturbance · post-operative state · myocardial ischaemia · obstructive sleep apnoea · mitral valve disease.** New AF in an unwell patient is frequently a symptom of the acute illness, and treating the illness often resolves the rhythm. **Checking the TSH in every new AF is a small habit that finds a treatable cause reasonably often.**

**S/Smx:** Palpitations, dyspnoea, fatigue, reduced exercise tolerance, chest discomfort, presyncope — or entirely asymptomatic and found incidentally, which is common and does not reduce the stroke risk. Irregularly irregular pulse with a pulse deficit.

**Ix:** ECG (*why:* confirms the rhythm and distinguishes AF from flutter and MAT; *what:* absent P waves with irregular ventricular response, or sawtooth flutter waves). **TFTs** (*why:* thyrotoxicosis is a specifically treatable cause and is easily missed in an older patient presenting only with AF; *what:* suppressed TSH). UEC, magnesium, FBC (*why:* electrolyte disturbance and anaemia as precipitants, and renal function determines DOAC dosing; *what:* potassium, magnesium, creatinine, haemoglobin). **Transthoracic echocardiography** (*why:* identifies valvular disease — particularly mitral stenosis, which changes the anticoagulant choice — assesses LV function and atrial size, and informs rhythm control decisions; *what:* valve lesions, LV function, atrial dimensions). Transoesophageal echocardiography before cardioversion where indicated (*why:* excludes left atrial appendage thrombus, which transthoracic imaging cannot reliably see; *what:* appendage thrombus). Ambulatory monitoring for paroxysmal AF (*why:* documents the rhythm and quantifies burden, and is relevant after cryptogenic stroke; *what:* AF episodes). Sleep study where OSA is suspected (*why:* untreated OSA substantially reduces the success of rhythm control and ablation; *what:* apnoea-hypopnoea index).

### 0.4.1 Mx – Immediate
Unstable → synchronised cardioversion. Stable → rate control, correct precipitants and electrolytes, and start the anticoagulation assessment.

### 0.4.2 Mx – Definitive
Rate or rhythm control strategy, anticoagulation per risk, and treatment of the underlying cause. Catheter ablation for symptomatic patients failing or declining drug therapy — **pulmonary vein isolation** being the standard approach. Left atrial appendage occlusion in patients who cannot be anticoagulated.

### 0.4.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
**Risk factor modification substantially reduces AF burden and improves ablation success** — weight loss, alcohol reduction, blood pressure control, treatment of sleep apnoea and exercise. This is genuinely disease-modifying and is under-emphasised relative to drugs and procedures. Annual review of anticoagulation, renal function and rate control.

---

## 0.5 Symptomatic Bradycardia

**D:** A heart rate below the normal range causing symptoms or haemodynamic compromise.

**R/Causes:**
**Physiological** — athletic training, sleep, vagal tone. **Drugs** — beta-blockers including eye drops, non-dihydropyridine calcium channel blockers, digoxin, amiodarone, clonidine, ivabradine, opioids, and cholinesterase inhibitors. **Ischaemia** — particularly inferior myocardial infarction, where sinus bradycardia and AV block are common, usually vagally mediated and often transient. **Degenerative conduction system disease** and sick sinus syndrome. **Metabolic** — hypothyroidism and myxoedema, hypothermia, **hyperkalaemia**, hypoxia. **Raised intracranial pressure** with the Cushing response. **Infection** — Lyme carditis, and aortic root abscess complicating endocarditis.

> [!info] AV block — the distinction that determines urgency
> **First degree** — prolonged PR, every beat conducts. Usually benign.
> **Second degree Mobitz I (Wenckebach)** — **progressive PR lengthening until a beat drops.** The block is usually **within the AV node**, it is often vagally mediated or drug-related, it responds to atropine, and it rarely progresses. Generally benign.
> **Second degree Mobitz II** — **constant PR interval with sudden unheralded dropped beats.** The block is **infranodal** (His-Purkinje). It is **not** reliably atropine-responsive, it progresses unpredictably to complete heart block, and it usually needs pacing. **This is the dangerous one.**
> **Third degree (complete)** — **AV dissociation** with an independent escape rhythm. A **narrow** escape suggests a junctional origin and relative stability; a **broad, slow** escape suggests a ventricular origin and is unstable.

> [!danger] Atropine may not work — and can make things worse
> Atropine acts by blocking vagal tone **at the AV node.** In **infranodal block** — Mobitz II and complete heart block with a broad escape — the AV node is not the problem, so atropine does not improve conduction. Worse, it can **increase the atrial rate without increasing conduction**, effectively increasing the degree of block.
> **Atropine is also ineffective in the transplanted (denervated) heart**, where there is no vagal innervation to block.
> In these situations move directly to **transcutaneous pacing and a chronotropic infusion**, and arrange transvenous pacing. `UNVERIFIED — atropine dose and maximum, infusion agents and rates, and transcutaneous pacing settings are omitted; obtain from ANZCOR Guideline 11.9.`

> [!warning] Transcutaneous pacing is painful and frequently ineffective
> It requires **adequate analgesia and sedation**, and **electrical capture must be confirmed mechanically** — a pacing spike followed by a QRS on the monitor does not mean the heart is contracting. **Feel for a pulse or confirm on echocardiography.** Failure to confirm mechanical capture is a common error that leads to a false sense of security in a patient who is not being perfused.

> [!tip] Bradycardia with a wide QRS — think potassium
> **Hyperkalaemia produces bradycardia with QRS widening**, peaked T waves and loss of P waves, progressing to a sine wave pattern and arrest. It is fast to check, immediately treatable, and will not respond to pacing. Check a gas.

**S/Smx:** Fatigue, exertional intolerance, dizziness, presyncope, syncope, confusion in the elderly, dyspnoea, chest pain, and — with adverse features — hypotension, heart failure and reduced conscious state.

**Ix:** 12-lead ECG and rhythm strip (*why:* classifies the block, which determines whether atropine will work and whether pacing is needed; *what:* PR relationship, dropped beats, AV dissociation, escape width). **VBG with potassium** (*why:* hyperkalaemia is a rapidly reversible cause that pacing will not address; *what:* potassium). **Full medication review including eye drops** (*why:* drug-induced bradycardia is the commonest reversible cause and topical beta-blockers are systemically absorbed and routinely overlooked; *what:* rate-limiting agents). Digoxin level (*why:* toxicity causes bradyarrhythmia and is treatable with specific antibody fragments; *what:* level in context of potassium and renal function). TFTs (*why:* hypothyroidism; *what:* raised TSH). Troponin and ECG for ischaemia (*why:* inferior MI causes AV block, and reperfusion may resolve it without pacing; *what:* inferior ST elevation). Core temperature (*why:* hypothermia; *what:* low temperature). Ambulatory monitoring where symptoms are intermittent (*why:* correlates symptoms with rhythm, which is the basis of a pacing decision in intermittent disease; *what:* symptom-rhythm correlation). Echocardiography (*why:* structural disease and, with fever, endocarditis with aortic root abscess, which is a surgical emergency presenting as new AV block; *what:* vegetations, abscess, LV function).

### 0.5.1 Mx – Immediate
Assess for adverse features. Oxygen, IV access, monitoring. **Correct reversible causes — potassium, drugs, hypothermia, hypoxia.** Atropine where the block is nodal. **Transcutaneous pacing with sedation** and confirmed mechanical capture, or a chronotropic infusion, where atropine fails or the block is infranodal. Arrange transvenous pacing and cardiology involvement.

### 0.5.2 Mx – Definitive
Cease the offending drug where possible. Reperfusion for ischaemic cause. **Permanent pacemaker** for irreversible symptomatic bradycardia, Mobitz II or complete heart block.

### 0.5.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
Device follow-up. Driving restrictions after syncope and after device implantation — cross-refer [[A10]] 0.6. Medication review at every subsequent presentation, since rate-limiting drugs are frequently restarted by another team.

---

## 0.6 Cardiac Device Events

**D:** Problems arising in patients with a permanent pacemaker, implantable cardioverter defibrillator (ICD) or cardiac resynchronisation device.

> [!danger] Electrical storm
> **Three or more appropriate ICD shocks within 24 hours.** This is a medical emergency with high mortality — the shocks are a symptom of recurrent ventricular arrhythmia, and the arrhythmia is the problem.
> Management involves treating the underlying trigger (ischaemia, electrolytes, heart failure decompensation, drug effect), **beta-blockade**, antiarrhythmic therapy, **sedation** — which is genuinely therapeutic, since sympathetic drive perpetuates the storm — and urgent cardiology and electrophysiology involvement. `UNVERIFIED — agents and doses.`

> [!warning] Inappropriate shocks
> Shocks delivered for a rhythm that is not ventricular arrhythmia — most often **AF with a rapid ventricular response**, sinus tachycardia, **lead fracture** or oversensing. They are extremely distressing and cause genuine psychological harm, and repeated inappropriate shocks are a common reason for presentation.
> **The device must be interrogated** to determine whether shocks were appropriate — this is not something that can be determined clinically, and it directs everything that follows.

> [!danger] Magnet response differs between pacemakers and ICDs — this is examined
> **A magnet placed over an ICD suspends tachyarrhythmia detection and therapy — it stops shocks. It does NOT affect the pacing function.**
> **A magnet placed over a pacemaker causes asynchronous pacing at a fixed magnet rate.** It does not "turn it off".
> Confusing these is dangerous. Applying a magnet to an ICD in a patient with genuine ongoing VT removes their protection. Applying one to a pacemaker expecting it to stop shocks achieves nothing.
> Magnet application to an ICD is used for **repeated inappropriate shocks**, in a monitored setting, with cardiology involvement, and the magnet must not be forgotten in place.

> [!tip] Device infection
> Presents as pocket pain, erythema, swelling, discharge or erosion, or as **bacteraemia and endocarditis** with no local signs at all. **Staphylococcal bacteraemia in a patient with any cardiac device should be assumed to involve the device until proven otherwise.**
> **Antibiotics alone rarely cure device infection — complete system removal, including the leads, is usually required.** Take blood cultures before antibiotics, obtain a transoesophageal echocardiogram, and involve cardiology and infectious diseases early.

> [!info] Other device problems worth recognising
> **Failure to capture** — pacing spikes with no following QRS. Lead displacement, fracture, exit block, or metabolic causes.
> **Failure to sense** — the device paces inappropriately because it does not see intrinsic beats, risking an R-on-T phenomenon.
> **Oversensing** — the device interprets noise, myopotentials or T waves as beats and inhibits pacing, which is dangerous in a pacing-dependent patient.
> **Twiddler's syndrome** — the patient manipulates the generator in its pocket, twisting and dislodging the leads.
> **Pacemaker syndrome** — symptoms of fatigue, dyspnoea and neck pulsation from loss of AV synchrony in single-chamber ventricular pacing.

> [!warning] Reading the ECG in a paced patient
> Right ventricular pacing produces a **left bundle branch block morphology**, which obscures the ST changes of myocardial infarction. The **Sgarbossa criteria** (and their modified version) are used to identify infarction in the presence of LBBB or ventricular pacing. `UNVERIFIED — criteria and point values.` Do not conclude "paced rhythm, uninterpretable" and stop — a patient with a device can still be having a STEMI.

**S/Smx:** Shocks (single or multiple), palpitations, syncope, dizziness, dyspnoea, pocket pain or swelling, fever, and hiccups or diaphragmatic twitching from phrenic nerve stimulation by a malpositioned lead.

**Ix:** **Device interrogation** (*why:* it is the definitive investigation — it records every arrhythmia, every therapy delivered, whether shocks were appropriate, and lead integrity, and no clinical assessment substitutes for it; *what:* stored electrograms, therapies delivered, lead impedance and thresholds). 12-lead ECG (*why:* identifies the underlying rhythm, capture and sensing problems, and ischaemia using Sgarbossa criteria; *what:* pacing spikes with or without capture, ST changes). CXR (*why:* lead position, fracture, displacement, and Twiddler's coiling are visible; *what:* lead integrity and position). UEC with potassium and magnesium (*why:* electrolyte disturbance both provokes arrhythmia and raises pacing thresholds; *what:* derangement). Troponin (*why:* ischaemia as the driver of an arrhythmic storm; *what:* elevation). **Blood cultures before antibiotics** where infection is suspected (*why:* organism identification determines management and the need for system removal; *what:* staphylococcal or other growth). Transoesophageal echocardiography for suspected device infection or endocarditis (*why:* transthoracic imaging poorly visualises leads and small vegetations; *what:* lead vegetations, valvular involvement). Chest and pocket examination (*why:* erosion and pocket infection are clinical diagnoses; *what:* erythema, fluctuance, erosion).

### 0.6.1 Mx – Immediate
Assess the patient, not just the device. Continuous monitoring. Treat the underlying arrhythmia or precipitant. **Magnet for repeated inappropriate ICD shocks, in a monitored setting, with cardiology contacted.** Sedation and beta-blockade for electrical storm. Blood cultures before antibiotics if infection is possible.

### 0.6.2 Mx – Definitive
Device interrogation and reprogramming. Lead revision. **Complete system extraction** for infection. Ablation for recurrent VT.

### 0.6.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
Regular device follow-up. **Psychological support after shocks** — anxiety and avoidance after an ICD shock are common, under-recognised and treatable. Driving restrictions after a shock or after implantation, which differ for appropriate and inappropriate therapy — cross-refer [[A10]] 0.6. Advance care planning, including **deactivation of ICD shock therapy at end of life**, which should be discussed proactively rather than during a terminal admission where an unsuspended device will shock a dying patient repeatedly.

> [!warning] Sourcing
> Written from model knowledge, not retrieved from guidelines. Discriminators and diagnostic reasoning are reliable at intern level. **Every threshold, timing figure, score component and dose carries an `UNVERIFIED` marker naming what to check, or has been omitted with the omission stated in place.** Verify orthostatic hypotension criteria, syncope risk scores and driving restrictions against current cardiology guidance, eTG, and **Austroads Assessing Fitness to Drive** respectively.

## 0.1 Transient Loss of Consciousness — The Framework

**D:** Complete loss of consciousness that is transient, self-limiting, of rapid onset, and followed by full spontaneous recovery.

> [!tip] Three categories, and the first branch is the whole assessment
> **1. Syncope** — global cerebral hypoperfusion. Reflex, orthostatic, or cardiac.
> **2. Seizure** — abnormal cortical electrical activity.
> **3. Other** — psychogenic non-epileptic events, hypoglycaemia, intoxication, head trauma, and the "not really TLoC" group such as falls without loss of consciousness.

> [!danger] The collateral history is the investigation
> **What a witness saw is worth more than any test in this presentation.** The patient by definition did not observe their own event. Ring the family member, the ambulance officer, the bystander. Ask specifically: how long were they out, what colour were they, did they jerk and for how long, were their eyes open or closed, how long before they were talking sensibly again, and what were they doing immediately beforehand.
> Failing to obtain a collateral history is the commonest reason this presentation goes unresolved.

> [!info] Syncope versus seizure
> | | Favours syncope | Favours seizure |
> |---|---|---|
> | **Trigger** | Standing, pain, heat, emotion, venepuncture | Sleep deprivation, alcohol, flashing lights, or none |
> | **Prodrome** | Nausea, sweating, warmth, **greying or tunnelling of vision**, ringing ears | Aura — déjà vu, epigastric rising, olfactory or focal sensory |
> | **Duration of LOC** | **Seconds** | **Minutes** |
> | **Colour** | **Pale, clammy** | Flushed or cyanosed |
> | **Movements** | Brief, few, irregular jerks **after** collapse | **Rhythmic, sustained**, often preceding or with onset of LOC |
> | **Tongue** | Tip, or not bitten | **Lateral tongue bite — highly specific** |
> | **Recovery** | **Rapid orientation**, often within seconds | **Prolonged postictal confusion**, drowsiness, headache |
> | **Head turning** | No | Forced head or eye deviation |

> [!danger] Convulsive syncope — the trap
> **Brief myoclonic jerking occurs in a substantial proportion of ordinary syncopal episodes**, caused by transient cerebral hypoperfusion, and it is frequently reported by witnesses as "a fit". Crucially, **the jerking in syncope follows the collapse**, is brief and irregular, and is not accompanied by prolonged postictal confusion.
> Patients are commonly and incorrectly labelled epileptic on this basis, started on antiepileptic drugs and have their licence removed. **The presence of jerking does not make it a seizure.** Ask about the sequence and the recovery, not just the movements.

> [!warning] Incontinence does not discriminate
> Urinary incontinence occurs in both syncope and seizure and is not a useful discriminator, despite being taught as one. **Lateral tongue biting and prolonged postictal confusion are the features that genuinely separate them.**

**S/Smx:** As above, plus circumstances — posture at onset, activity, relation to exertion, meals, medication changes, and preceding palpitations or chest pain.

**Ix:** **12-lead ECG in every patient with TLoC** (*why:* it is cheap, immediate, and the only routine test that identifies the conditions that kill — long QT, Brugada, pre-excitation, conduction disease, prior infarction and hypertrophy; *what:* see the ECG hunt list in 0.2). Bedside glucose (*why:* hypoglycaemia causes both loss of consciousness and seizure and is instantly reversible; *what:* low glucose). **Lying and standing blood pressure**, measured properly (*why:* orthostatic hypotension is common, easily demonstrated and routinely not measured because it takes a few minutes; *what:* postural fall). FBC (*why:* anaemia and occult GI bleeding present as syncope; *what:* haemoglobin). UEC and electrolytes (*why:* volume depletion and electrolyte disturbance; *what:* derangement). Pregnancy test in a woman of reproductive age (*why:* **ruptured ectopic pregnancy presents as collapse** and is rapidly fatal; *what:* positive test). Troponin only where ischaemia is clinically suspected (*why:* routine troponin in syncope generates more confusion than diagnosis — cross-refer [[B1]] 0.5; *what:* elevation in context). Further testing directed by the category identified — see 0.2.

### 0.1.1 Mx – Immediate
A–E, glucose, ECG, lying and standing blood pressure, collateral history. Look for and treat injury sustained in the collapse.

### 0.1.2 Mx – Definitive
Category-specific, per 0.2.

### 0.1.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
**Driving advice, given and documented at the first presentation** — cross-refer [[A10]] 0.6. Non-driving periods differ for syncope and seizure, for treated and untreated causes, and for private and commercial licences.

---

## 0.2 Syncope

**D:** Transient loss of consciousness due to **global cerebral hypoperfusion**, with rapid onset, short duration and spontaneous complete recovery.

**A/P:** Cerebral perfusion depends on mean arterial pressure, which is cardiac output multiplied by systemic vascular resistance. A fall in either, sufficient to drop cerebral blood flow below the autoregulatory floor for a matter of seconds, causes loss of consciousness. **Lying flat restores perfusion, which is why syncope is self-terminating** — and why holding a fainting patient upright, or sitting them in a chair, prolongs the episode and is a common bystander and staff error.

> [!tip] The three categories
> **Reflex (neurally mediated)** — the commonest, and generally benign.
> · **Vasovagal**: a clear trigger (prolonged standing, heat, pain, fear, the sight of blood, venepuncture), a **prodrome** of nausea, sweating, warmth and visual greying, and rapid recovery.
> · **Situational**: cough, micturition, defecation, swallowing, laughing, or post-exercise.
> · **Carotid sinus hypersensitivity**: older patients, sometimes triggered by head turning, tight collars or shaving.
> **Orthostatic** — occurs on standing, within seconds to minutes.
> **Cardiac** — arrhythmic or structural. **The dangerous category.**

> [!danger] Red flags for cardiac syncope
> **· Syncope DURING exertion** — as opposed to immediately after, which is more often reflex. **Exertional syncope is aortic stenosis or hypertrophic cardiomyopathy until proven otherwise**, and it warrants urgent assessment.
> **· Syncope while supine or sitting** — reflex syncope essentially requires an upright posture.
> **· No prodrome — sudden collapse without warning**, particularly with facial or dental injury, which indicates no protective reflex time.
> **· Palpitations immediately preceding.**
> **· Known structural heart disease, heart failure or prior myocardial infarction.**
> **· An abnormal ECG.**
> **· Family history of sudden cardiac death under 40, or of drowning or unexplained single-vehicle accident** — these are how channelopathy deaths are frequently recorded.
> Any of these moves the patient out of "probable vasovagal, discharge home".

> [!warning] The ECG hunt list in syncope
> Look deliberately for: **prolonged QT** · **short QT** · **Brugada pattern** (coved ST elevation in V1–V2) · **delta wave** of Wolff-Parkinson-White · **epsilon waves or T inversion in V1–V3** suggesting arrhythmogenic right ventricular cardiomyopathy · **deep Q waves and voltage criteria** suggesting hypertrophic cardiomyopathy · **bifascicular block or first degree AV block** suggesting intermittent complete heart block · prior infarction. `UNVERIFIED — QT correction formulae and cut-offs, and Brugada morphology criteria.`
> These are not subtle once you are looking for them, and they are missed when the ECG is glanced at for rate and rhythm only.

> [!info] Orthostatic hypotension
> Measured as a fall in blood pressure on standing from supine, assessed at intervals over several minutes — **many falls only appear after two or three minutes**, so an immediate single reading misses them. `UNVERIFIED — the diagnostic fall in systolic and diastolic pressure, and the timing of measurements, require verification.`
> **Drugs are the commonest cause** — antihypertensives, alpha-blockers for prostatic symptoms, diuretics, nitrates, tricyclics, antipsychotics, and opioids — and the medication list is the first place to look. Also: volume depletion, prolonged bed rest, and **autonomic failure** in Parkinson's disease, diabetes, multiple system atrophy and amyloidosis.

**S/Smx:** Prodrome or its absence, posture, trigger, duration, recovery speed, injury, and associated palpitations or chest pain. Examine for murmurs (aortic stenosis, and the dynamic murmur of HOCM), postural blood pressure change, and signs of anaemia or volume depletion.

**Ix:** As per 0.1, plus: **echocardiography** where structural disease is suspected, there is a murmur, or the ECG is abnormal (*why:* identifies aortic stenosis, hypertrophic cardiomyopathy and impaired ventricular function, the structural causes of exertional and life-threatening syncope; *what:* valve gradients, septal thickness with outflow obstruction, ejection fraction). **Ambulatory ECG monitoring**, with duration matched to symptom frequency (*why:* the diagnosis requires correlating symptoms with rhythm, and a 24-hour monitor in a patient with monthly episodes is close to useless — extended monitoring or an implantable loop recorder is required for infrequent events; *what:* symptom-rhythm correlation). Exercise testing where syncope was exertional (*why:* reproduces the circumstances and identifies exercise-induced arrhythmia and outflow obstruction; *what:* arrhythmia, blood pressure response). Tilt table testing in selected recurrent reflex syncope (*why:* confirms a reflex mechanism where the diagnosis is uncertain and reassurance is not sufficient; *what:* reproduced syncope with characteristic haemodynamics). Carotid sinus massage in older patients, **with cardiac monitoring and after excluding carotid bruit or recent stroke** (*why:* diagnoses carotid sinus hypersensitivity, which is pacemaker-treatable; *what:* pause or blood pressure fall). Risk stratification with a validated tool such as the Canadian Syncope Risk Score (*why:* structures the admit-versus-discharge decision more reliably than impression; *what:* risk category — `UNVERIFIED — components and thresholds`). **Genetic and family screening** where an inherited condition is suspected (*why:* the patient's relatives may be at risk of sudden death and the index presentation is the opportunity to find them; *what:* family cardiac assessment).

### 0.2.1 Mx – Immediate
Lie the patient flat with legs elevated. Assess for injury. ECG, glucose, postural blood pressure. Admit and monitor where red flags are present.

### 0.2.2 Mx – Definitive
**Reflex syncope:** explanation and reassurance, trigger avoidance, adequate hydration and salt, **counterpressure manoeuvres** (leg crossing with muscle tensing, arm tensing, handgrip) taught explicitly — these genuinely abort episodes if started during the prodrome and are the single most useful intervention. Review and reduce contributing medications.
**Orthostatic:** rationalise the medication list first, then compression stockings, gradual position change, adequate fluid and salt, and pharmacological options if needed.
**Cardiac:** treat the arrhythmia or structural lesion. Pacemaker, ICD, valve intervention or ablation as indicated.

### 0.2.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
Driving advice. Occupational advice for those working at height, with machinery, or in safety-critical roles. Family screening for inherited conditions. Reassurance in reflex syncope is genuinely therapeutic — **patients frequently believe they have a serious heart problem, and explaining the mechanism reduces both anxiety and recurrence.**

---

## 0.3 Presyncope

**D:** The sensation of impending loss of consciousness — lightheadedness, greying vision, weakness — without actually losing it.

> [!danger] Presyncope has the same causes and the same significance as syncope
> **It is not a lesser problem.** Whether the patient lost consciousness or merely nearly did may reflect nothing more than whether they happened to sit down in time. **Investigate presyncope with the same red flags, the same ECG scrutiny and the same threshold as syncope**, including exertional presyncope and presyncope with palpitations.
> This is a common and consequential under-triage.

> [!warning] Distinguish presyncope from vertigo and from disequilibrium
> Patients use "dizzy" for at least four different things, and the word alone is uninformative. Ask them to describe it without using the word:
> **· Presyncope** — "about to faint", greying vision, going down.
> **· Vertigo** — an illusion of movement, spinning of self or surroundings. Points to vestibular pathology — cross-refer [[D5]].
> **· Disequilibrium** — unsteadiness on the feet with a clear head. Points to gait, proprioceptive or cerebellar problems.
> **· Non-specific lightheadedness** — often anxiety, hyperventilation, or medication.
> Getting this distinction right redirects the entire workup, and it costs one well-phrased question.

**S/Smx:** Lightheadedness, visual greying or tunnelling, ringing in the ears, nausea, sweating, weakness, and the sense of needing to sit or lie down.

**Ix:** As for syncope in 0.2 — the workup is the same. Particular attention to lying and standing blood pressure, the ECG, haemoglobin and the medication list.

### 0.3.1 Mx – Immediate
Lie flat, assess as for syncope.

### 0.3.2 Mx – Definitive
Cause-specific, as per 0.2.

### 0.3.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
As per 0.2, including driving advice where the episodes are unpredictable.

---

## 0.4 Conscious Collapse and the Unwitnessed Fall

**D:** A fall to the ground without clear loss of consciousness, or a fall where whether consciousness was lost is unknown.

> [!danger] "Mechanical fall" is a diagnosis of exclusion, not a triage category
> An older person who falls has an underlying reason until proven otherwise, and labelling it mechanical at the front door closes the assessment prematurely. **The fall may be the presentation of syncope, arrhythmia, sepsis, myocardial infarction, stroke, hypoglycaemia, anaemia, dehydration, a new medication, or a subdural haematoma.**
> **If the fall was unwitnessed and the patient does not clearly recall hitting the ground, assume syncope and investigate accordingly.** Patients frequently have no memory of losing consciousness and will honestly report that they simply tripped.

> [!warning] The long lie
> An older person on the floor for hours accumulates **rhabdomyolysis, acute kidney injury, hypothermia, dehydration, pressure injury and aspiration** — cross-refer [[A6]] 0.5 and [[A7]] 0.6. **Ask how long they were down**, and check a CK, potassium, renal function and temperature in anyone who was there for a prolonged period.
> The long lie is also a strong marker of social vulnerability, and it should trigger a conversation about living arrangements, alarms and support.

> [!tip] The multifactorial falls assessment
> Falls in older people are almost always multifactorial. Assess: **gait and balance** · **vision** · **cognition** · **medications**, particularly the number of them and any sedatives, antihypertensives, opioids and anticholinergics · **postural blood pressure** · **feet and footwear** · **continence**, since rushing to the toilet at night is a classic mechanism · **home hazards** · **vitamin D, calcium and bone health** · **alcohol** · and **fear of falling**, which itself reduces activity and worsens the risk.
> Each of these is modifiable, which is the point of listing them.

**S/Smx:** Circumstances of the fall, whether consciousness was lost, prodrome, time on the floor, injuries, and the presence of any preceding illness or medication change.

**Ix:** **CK, UEC and potassium** where the patient was down for a prolonged period (*why:* rhabdomyolysis and hyperkalaemia are common after a long lie and will be missed unless specifically sought; *what:* raised CK, rising creatinine, hyperkalaemia). Core temperature (*why:* hypothermia after a long lie occurs indoors and is easily missed; *what:* low temperature). ECG and postural blood pressure (*why:* the fall may be syncope; *what:* arrhythmia, conduction disease, postural drop). Bedside glucose (*why:* hypoglycaemia; *what:* low glucose). FBC, CRP, urinalysis and cultures (*why:* infection presents in the elderly as a fall with no other symptom; *what:* infective source). **CT head with a low threshold, particularly if anticoagulated** (*why:* subdural and intracranial haemorrhage after apparently minor head trauma in an anticoagulated older person, and delayed presentation is common; *what:* haemorrhage — cross-refer [[F0.5]] 0.8). Radiography of any painful area, with specific attention to the hip (*why:* **an impacted or undisplaced neck of femur fracture may allow weight-bearing and be missed on plain films**, so persistent pain warrants further imaging; *what:* fracture). Medication review (*why:* the commonest modifiable contributor; *what:* culprit drugs). Bone health assessment (*why:* the fall that caused a fragility fracture predicts the next one, and osteoporosis is under-treated after fractures; *what:* fracture risk, vitamin D).

### 0.4.1 Mx – Immediate
Assess and treat injuries. Investigate for the cause. Rehydrate and correct electrolytes. Analgesia.

### 0.4.2 Mx – Definitive
Treat the identified cause. Orthopaedic management of fractures.

### 0.4.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
**Multifactorial falls intervention** — exercise programs with a balance component have good evidence, alongside medication review, home assessment by occupational therapy, vision correction, and bone protection. Cross-refer [[18_Geriatrics_and_Older_Persons_Health]]. Consider a personal alarm where the patient lives alone and has had a long lie.

---

## 0.5 Hypotension

**D:** Blood pressure below the range required to perfuse organs adequately — a functional definition rather than a numerical one.

> [!danger] Hypotension is relative
> **A blood pressure that is normal on paper may be profoundly hypotensive for a patient whose usual pressure is much higher.** A chronically hypertensive patient perfusing at 180 systolic may be shocked at 110. Conversely, a small young woman may live comfortably at 90.
> **Compare against the patient's own baseline**, which is usually available in the record or from the GP, rather than against a population range. Failure to do this delays recognition of shock in exactly the patients who tolerate it worst.

**A/P:** Blood pressure is the product of cardiac output and systemic vascular resistance. Hypotension therefore reflects a fall in preload, pump function, or vascular tone — which maps onto the shock phenotypes and their bedside signatures. Cross-refer [[F0.3]] 0.1 for the full phenotype framework, which is not repeated here.

> [!tip] Causes worth separating
> **Hypovolaemic** — haemorrhage, dehydration, third-spacing, burns.
> **Cardiogenic** — infarction, arrhythmia, valvular failure, cardiomyopathy.
> **Obstructive** — tamponade, tension pneumothorax, massive PE.
> **Distributive** — sepsis, anaphylaxis, neurogenic, **adrenal insufficiency.**
> **Iatrogenic** — this deserves separate emphasis on the ward, since it is the commonest cause of an inpatient hypotension call: antihypertensives given to a patient who is now dry or septic, opioids and sedatives, spinal and epidural anaesthesia, diuretics continued through an acute illness, and rapid infusion of certain drugs.

> [!warning] Hypotension refractory to fluid and vasopressors — think of these
> **Adrenal insufficiency** — cortisol is required for vascular responsiveness to catecholamines, and its absence produces vasoplegia that does not respond to noradrenaline. Look for hyponatraemia with hyperkalaemia and hypoglycaemia, and ask about steroid use or recent cessation. Cross-refer [[F0.3]] 0.6.
> **Tension pneumothorax and tamponade** — obstruction that no volume of fluid will overcome.
> **Severe acidosis** — impairs catecholamine responsiveness.
> **Anaphylaxis** — including biphasic and delayed presentations.
> **Occult haemorrhage** — retroperitoneal, intra-abdominal, or into a fractured limb.

**S/Smx:** Dizziness, weakness, confusion, oliguria, cool peripheries or warm vasodilated ones depending on the phenotype, and prolonged capillary refill. **Mental state and urine output are more informative than the number.**

**Ix:** Comparison with the patient's baseline blood pressure (*why:* determines whether this reading represents shock for this individual; *what:* usual pressure from the record or the GP). Serial lactate (*why:* the objective marker of whether the pressure is producing hypoperfusion, which is the question that matters; *what:* elevation and clearance). Bedside echocardiography (*why:* phenotypes the hypotension in under a minute — contractility, RV size, pericardial fluid, IVC calibre; *what:* the four-way answer). ECG (*why:* arrhythmia and infarction; *what:* rhythm, ischaemia). FBC (*why:* occult bleeding; *what:* haemoglobin trend). UEC with **sodium, potassium and glucose** (*why:* the hyponatraemia-hyperkalaemia-hypoglycaemia triad points at adrenal insufficiency; *what:* the pattern). Cortisol where adrenal crisis is possible, **taken before hydrocortisone but never delaying it** (*why:* the sample is unobtainable once treatment starts; *what:* inappropriately low cortisol). Septic screen (*why:* sepsis is the commonest distributive cause; *what:* source). **Full medication chart review including timing of the last doses** (*why:* iatrogenic hypotension is the commonest inpatient cause and is immediately reversible by not giving the next dose; *what:* antihypertensives, opioids, sedatives, diuretics). CXR (*why:* pneumothorax, pulmonary oedema; *what:* findings).

### 0.5.1 Mx – Immediate
A–E, lie flat with legs elevated, oxygen, large-bore access, fluid challenge with **reassessment after each bolus** rather than a prescribed volume. Identify the phenotype. **Withhold the contributing drugs** — this is the fastest intervention available on a ward and is frequently overlooked while fluid is being run.

### 0.5.2 Mx – Definitive
Phenotype-specific — cross-refer [[F0.3]]. Vasopressors and ICU where fluid-unresponsive. Hydrocortisone where adrenal insufficiency is plausible, given empirically rather than after confirmation.

### 0.5.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
**Review the antihypertensive regimen** — an episode of hypotension in an older patient is a signal that the regimen may be too aggressive for their current state, and deprescribing is an active intervention rather than a failure. Cross-refer [[18_Geriatrics_and_Older_Persons_Health]] Polypharmacy and Deprescribing.

---

> [!note] Cross-references
> Shock phenotypes in full → [[F0.3]] 0.1–0.6 · Arrhythmia and bradycardia → [[B3]] · Aortic stenosis and murmurs → [[B5]] · Seizure and its differential → [[D6]] · Vertigo and disequilibrium → [[D5]] · Head injury on anticoagulants → [[F0.5]] 0.7–0.8 · Long lie, hypothermia and rhabdomyolysis → [[A6]] 0.5 and [[A7]] 0.6 · Adrenal crisis → [[F0.3]] 0.6 · Driving after syncope → [[A10]] 0.6 · Falls in the elderly → [[18_Geriatrics_and_Older_Persons_Health]]


<!-- ===== SOURCE: B5_Murmurs__Endocarditis_and_Vascular_Disease.md ===== -->


> [!warning] Sourcing
> Written from model knowledge, not retrieved from guidelines. Examination reasoning, mechanism and discriminators are reliable at intern level. **Every threshold, grading value, time window, dose and criterion carries an `UNVERIFIED` marker naming what to check, or has been omitted with the omission stated in place.** Verify Duke criteria and endocarditis regimens against eTG Antibiotic, ankle-brachial index thresholds and revascularisation criteria against current vascular guidance, and peripartum cardiomyopathy management against SOMANZ and cardio-obstetric sources.

---

## 0.1 Heart Murmurs — Assessment

**D:** An audible sound generated by turbulent blood flow across a valve, a septal defect, or a high-flow state.

> [!danger] All diastolic murmurs are pathological. All continuous murmurs are pathological.
> There is no such thing as an innocent diastolic murmur. A diastolic murmur warrants echocardiography regardless of how well the patient appears.
> **Innocent systolic murmurs** are soft, early or mid-systolic, without radiation or thrill, with normal pulses, normal heart sounds and no symptoms — and they are common, particularly in children, pregnancy, anaemia, fever and thyrotoxicosis, all of which are high-output states.

> [!tip] What to characterise
> **Timing** — systolic, diastolic, or continuous, and where within the cycle.
> **Site of maximal intensity** and **radiation** — aortic stenosis to the carotids, mitral regurgitation to the axilla.
> **Character** — ejection (crescendo-decrescendo), pansystolic (uniform), blowing, rumbling, harsh.
> **Intensity** — graded 1 to 6, with **grade 4 and above defined by a palpable thrill**, which is the practically useful boundary. `UNVERIFIED — Levine grading descriptors.`
> **Associated findings** — pulse character, pulse pressure, apex beat, heart sounds, added sounds, and signs of heart failure. **The peripheral findings frequently establish the diagnosis more reliably than the murmur itself.**

> [!info] Dynamic manoeuvres
> **Inspiration increases venous return to the right heart, so right-sided murmurs get louder** — the mnemonic **RILE** (Right on Inspiration, Left on Expiration).
> **Valsalva and standing reduce preload, so most murmurs get quieter — with two exceptions that get LOUDER: hypertrophic cardiomyopathy and mitral valve prolapse.** Reduced ventricular volume worsens outflow obstruction in HOCM and increases prolapse in MVP. This pair of exceptions is examined repeatedly.
> **Squatting and handgrip increase afterload and preload**, increasing mitral regurgitation, ventricular septal defect and aortic regurgitation murmurs, and reducing the HOCM murmur.

> [!tip] The common lesions
> | Lesion | Murmur | Key associated findings |
> |---|---|---|
> | **Aortic stenosis** | Ejection systolic, right sternal edge, **radiates to carotids** | **Slow-rising pulse, narrow pulse pressure**, heaving apex, soft S2. Triad: **angina, syncope, dyspnoea** |
> | **Mitral regurgitation** | **Pansystolic**, apex, **radiates to axilla** | Displaced apex, soft S1, S3 |
> | **Aortic regurgitation** | **Early diastolic**, left sternal edge, **sitting forward in expiration** | **Collapsing pulse, wide pulse pressure**, displaced apex |
> | **Mitral stenosis** | **Mid-diastolic rumble** at apex, in left lateral position | **Loud S1, opening snap**, malar flush, AF |
> | **HOCM** | Ejection systolic, **louder on Valsalva** | Jerky pulse, family history of sudden death |
> | **VSD** | Harsh pansystolic, left sternal edge, thrill | Smaller defects are louder |

> [!warning] Murmurs in children
> Innocent murmurs are very common. **Red flags requiring assessment: any diastolic murmur · a harsh or loud murmur or a thrill · cyanosis · failure to thrive or poor feeding · absent or delayed femoral pulses (coarctation) · abnormal second heart sound · family history of congenital heart disease.**
> **Feel the femoral pulses in every child with a murmur** — coarctation is missed for years by not doing so.

**S/Smx:** Frequently asymptomatic. Otherwise dyspnoea, exertional intolerance, chest pain, syncope, palpitations, oedema, or the systemic features of endocarditis.

**Ix:** **Transthoracic echocardiography** (*why:* the definitive investigation — it identifies the lesion, quantifies severity, assesses ventricular consequences and determines whether intervention is needed, and auscultation alone cannot grade severity; *what:* valve morphology, gradients, regurgitant severity, chamber size, ejection fraction). ECG (*why:* left ventricular hypertrophy in aortic stenosis and HOCM, atrial fibrillation and P mitrale in mitral stenosis, and conduction disease suggesting an aortic root abscess in endocarditis; *what:* hypertrophy, AF, new AV block). CXR (*why:* chamber enlargement, pulmonary congestion, valve calcification; *what:* cardiomegaly, oedema, calcification). FBC (*why:* anaemia causes a flow murmur and worsens any valve lesion; *what:* haemoglobin). TFTs (*why:* thyrotoxicosis is a high-output state producing murmurs and AF; *what:* suppressed TSH). **Blood cultures before antibiotics in anyone with a murmur and fever** (*why:* endocarditis is the diagnosis that must not be missed and cultures taken after antibiotics may never grow; *what:* organism — see 0.2).

### 0.1.1 Mx – Immediate
Only if the patient is acutely unwell — see 0.2.

### 0.1.2 Mx – Definitive
Echocardiography and cardiology referral for any pathological murmur. Intervention decisions by severity, symptoms and ventricular consequences.

### 0.1.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
Surveillance echocardiography at intervals determined by lesion severity. **Endocarditis prophylaxis** for defined high-risk lesions and procedures — the indications are much narrower than historically taught. `UNVERIFIED — current prophylaxis indications and regimens from eTG Antibiotic; these have been substantially restricted.`

---

## 0.2 New and Changing Murmur

**D:** A murmur that is genuinely new, or that has changed in character or intensity — which in an unwell patient carries specific and urgent implications.

> [!danger] A new murmur in a febrile patient is infective endocarditis until proven otherwise
> **Take three sets of blood cultures from separate sites before antibiotics, and arrange echocardiography.** The combination of fever and a new murmur is one of the highest-yield clinical pairings in medicine.
> Risk groups: **prosthetic valves · previous endocarditis · congenital heart disease · injecting drug use** (characteristically right-sided, tricuspid, with septic pulmonary emboli and a relatively normal cardiac examination) · **rheumatic heart disease**, which in Australia disproportionately affects **Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander people**, among whom rates are among the highest in the world — cross-refer [[F0.5]] 0.10 · intravascular devices and recent instrumentation.
> Diagnosis uses the **modified Duke criteria**, combining microbiological, echocardiographic and clinical elements. `UNVERIFIED — the criteria, their weighting, and all antibiotic regimens and durations from eTG Antibiotic.`
> **Peripheral stigmata** — splinter haemorrhages, Osler nodes, Janeway lesions, Roth spots — are classically taught, genuinely uncommon in modern practice, and their absence means nothing.
> **A new AV block in endocarditis suggests an aortic root abscess** extending into the conduction system, and it is a surgical emergency. Watch the ECG.

> [!danger] Mechanical complications of myocardial infarction — days 3 to 7
> A new murmur in a patient several days after an infarct is a surgical emergency until proven otherwise.
> **Papillary muscle rupture** → acute severe mitral regurgitation with flash pulmonary oedema and cardiogenic shock. **The murmur may be soft or absent**, because the regurgitant pressure gradient equalises rapidly across a low-compliance left atrium — so a quiet murmur is not reassurance.
> **Ventricular septal rupture** → harsh pansystolic murmur with a thrill, and shock.
> **Free wall rupture** → tamponade and pulseless electrical activity.
> All three require urgent echocardiography and immediate cardiothoracic involvement. Cross-refer [[F0.3]] 0.3–0.4.

> [!warning] Other causes of a genuinely new murmur
> **Aortic dissection involving the root** → acute aortic regurgitation with chest pain — cross-refer [[B1]] 0.1. **Prosthetic valve thrombosis or dehiscence** → a new or altered murmur with muffled prosthetic sounds. **A high-output state** — anaemia, fever, thyrotoxicosis, pregnancy — producing a flow murmur that resolves with the cause. **Papillary muscle dysfunction** from ischaemia without frank rupture.

**S/Smx:** Fever, night sweats, weight loss, malaise and embolic phenomena in endocarditis; acute dyspnoea and shock in mechanical complications; chest pain in dissection.

**Ix:** **Three sets of blood cultures from separate venepuncture sites before antibiotics** (*why:* endocarditis is a continuous bacteraemia so separate sets increase yield and support the Duke criteria, and yield falls sharply once antibiotics start — in a stable patient it is worth waiting; *what:* organism and sensitivities). **Transthoracic echocardiography, proceeding to transoesophageal** (*why:* TTE has limited sensitivity for vegetations, particularly on prosthetic valves and for aortic root abscess, and a normal TTE does not exclude endocarditis — TOE is required where suspicion persists; *what:* vegetation, abscess, valve destruction, regurgitation, mechanical complication). Serial ECG (*why:* **new conduction disease signals aortic root abscess**; *what:* new AV block, lengthening PR). FBC, CRP, UEC, urinalysis (*why:* anaemia of chronic infection, inflammatory markers for monitoring, renal impairment and haematuria from immune complex glomerulonephritis or emboli; *what:* anaemia, raised CRP, haematuria). Imaging for embolic complications — CT brain, abdomen, and CT chest for septic pulmonary emboli in right-sided disease (*why:* embolic events change management and may need intervention; *what:* infarcts, abscesses, mycotic aneurysms). Troponin and urgent echocardiography where the murmur is post-infarct (*why:* mechanical complications are time-critical and surgical; *what:* wall motion, regurgitant jet, septal defect).

### 0.2.1 Mx – Immediate
Cultures before antibiotics. Empirical therapy once cultures are taken in a septic patient. Urgent echocardiography. **Cardiothoracic involvement immediately for post-infarct mechanical complications**, and cardiology plus infectious diseases for endocarditis.

### 0.2.2 Mx – Definitive
Prolonged targeted intravenous antibiotics for endocarditis, with surgery for heart failure, abscess, large vegetations with embolic risk, prosthetic valve involvement or uncontrolled infection. Urgent surgical repair for post-infarct rupture.

### 0.2.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
Dental review and oral health optimisation after endocarditis, which is under-arranged. Patient education about future prophylaxis and about seeking assessment for unexplained fever. **Secondary prophylaxis and follow-up in rheumatic heart disease**, with register-based recall — cross-refer [[F0.5]] 0.10. Drug and alcohol support where injecting drug use is the risk factor.

---

## 0.3 Peripartum Cardiomyopathy

**D:** Heart failure with reduced left ventricular ejection fraction developing towards the end of pregnancy or in the months following delivery, without another identifiable cause.

**R:**
*Unmodifiable:* Multiparity, multiple gestation, older maternal age, African ancestry, family history.
*Modifiable:* Pre-eclampsia and hypertensive disorders of pregnancy, obesity, prolonged tocolytic use, anaemia, malnutrition.

**A/P:** The mechanism is incompletely understood but involves **oxidative stress generating an antiangiogenic fragment of prolactin**, which damages the myocardial microvasculature, in a background of the profound haemodynamic load of pregnancy — increased plasma volume, increased cardiac output and reduced systemic vascular resistance. This prolactin hypothesis is the basis for investigational bromocriptine therapy.

> [!danger] The diagnosis is missed because the symptoms are the symptoms of pregnancy
> **Breathlessness, fatigue, ankle swelling and reduced exercise tolerance are normal in late pregnancy and the puerperium** — which is exactly why a failing heart is attributed to being pregnant. Women have died from this having been reassured repeatedly.
> **Features that should prompt an echocardiogram rather than reassurance: orthopnoea and paroxysmal nocturnal dyspnoea · dyspnoea at rest · a resting tachycardia that does not settle · a new murmur or third heart sound · raised JVP · hypoxia · symptoms that are worsening rather than plateauing · and any of these in a woman with pre-eclampsia.**
> The threshold for echocardiography in a breathless peripartum woman should be low.

> [!warning] The differential in a breathless peripartum woman
> **Pulmonary embolism** — pregnancy and the puerperium are prothrombotic. **Amniotic fluid embolism.** **Pre-eclampsia with pulmonary oedema.** **Pre-existing or undiagnosed valvular disease unmasked by pregnancy** — particularly mitral stenosis, where the increased cardiac output and heart rate are poorly tolerated. **Anaemia.** **Thyrotoxicosis.** All are serious and all present the same way.

**S/Smx:** Dyspnoea, orthopnoea, PND, fatigue, palpitations, cough, oedema, and in severe cases pulmonary oedema, arrhythmia or cardiogenic shock. Examination: tachycardia, raised JVP, third heart sound, crackles, displaced apex, murmur of functional mitral regurgitation.

**Ix:** **Echocardiography** (*why:* the diagnostic test — it demonstrates the reduced ejection fraction and excludes valvular and other structural causes, and there is no substitute; *what:* reduced LVEF, chamber dilatation, functional regurgitation, thrombus). **BNP or NT-proBNP** (*why:* a normal level makes heart failure unlikely, which is useful given how non-specific the symptoms are, though levels rise modestly in normal pregnancy; *what:* elevation). ECG (*why:* arrhythmia, ischaemia, and non-specific changes; *what:* tachycardia, arrhythmia, ST changes). CXR with abdominal shielding (*why:* pulmonary oedema and cardiomegaly, and the radiation dose is small and should not be withheld where indicated; *what:* oedema, cardiomegaly). FBC, UEC, LFT, TFTs (*why:* anaemia, renal impairment, thyroid disease, and the haemolysis and transaminitis of HELLP; *what:* derangement). Blood pressure and urine protein (*why:* pre-eclampsia coexists frequently and changes management; *what:* hypertension, proteinuria). Troponin (*why:* excludes ischaemic cause including spontaneous coronary artery dissection, which occurs peripartum; *what:* elevation). Investigation for PE where suspected (*why:* the principal differential; *what:* filling defect).

### 0.3.1 Mx – Immediate
Oxygen, sit upright, and manage acute pulmonary oedema — cross-refer [[F0.5]] 0.3. **Involve obstetrics, cardiology, anaesthetics and neonatology together**; this is a multidisciplinary condition and delivery timing is a joint decision.

### 0.3.2 Mx – Definitive
Standard heart failure therapy **adjusted for pregnancy and lactation** — **ACE inhibitors and ARBs are contraindicated antenatally** and alternatives are used, with the regimen changing after delivery and again depending on breastfeeding. **Anticoagulation is considered where the ejection fraction is very low**, given the elevated thromboembolic risk in an already prothrombotic state. Bromocriptine has been used investigationally on the prolactin hypothesis. `UNVERIFIED — every agent, its safety in pregnancy and lactation, the ejection fraction threshold for anticoagulation, and the status of bromocriptine require verification against SOMANZ and current cardio-obstetric guidance.`

### 0.3.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
**Serial echocardiography** — a substantial proportion recover ventricular function, and the degree of recovery determines both prognosis and advice about future pregnancy.
**Counselling about subsequent pregnancy is essential and is frequently not given.** Recurrence risk is significant, and it is higher in women whose ejection fraction has not normalised. This conversation should happen before the woman is pregnant again, with contraception discussed in the interim. Cross-refer [[O6]] Contraception.

---

## 0.4 Post-Catheterisation Vascular Complications

**D:** Local vascular injury following arterial access, most often femoral, for coronary or peripheral intervention.

**A/P:** Arterial puncture creates a defect that normally seals. Failure to seal produces a **pseudoaneurysm** — a contained pulsatile haematoma communicating with the arterial lumen through a neck, contained only by surrounding tissue rather than by a true vessel wall. An accompanying venous injury produces an **arteriovenous fistula.** A puncture made **above the inguinal ligament** bleeds into the retroperitoneum, where there is no tamponading tissue and no visible swelling.

> [!danger] Retroperitoneal haematoma — the one with no visible signs
> **A high femoral puncture bleeds into the retroperitoneum, and there may be NO groin swelling, NO bruise and NO local tenderness.**
> The presentation is **back, flank or abdominal pain, hypotension, tachycardia and a falling haemoglobin** in a patient hours after a procedure. It is frequently attributed to sedation, vasovagal reaction or analgesia effect while the patient exsanguinates.
> **Any hypotension after arterial access is retroperitoneal haemorrhage until excluded**, and the investigation is a CT abdomen. Cross-refer [[A9]] 0.1.

> [!tip] Pseudoaneurysm
> **A pulsatile groin mass with a systolic bruit or palpable thrill, and pain**, developing over hours to days after the procedure. Confirmed by **duplex ultrasound**, which shows the characteristic to-and-fro flow in the neck and swirling "yin-yang" flow in the sac.
> Management depends on size: small ones may thrombose spontaneously with observation; larger ones are treated with **ultrasound-guided thrombin injection** or, less commonly now, compression; surgery is reserved for failure, infection, rapid expansion, distal ischaemia or skin compromise. `UNVERIFIED — size thresholds and the technique details.`

> [!warning] The other access complications
> **Arteriovenous fistula** — a **continuous** bruit, since flow occurs in both systole and diastole. **Haematoma** — common, usually managed conservatively, but monitor for expansion and for compartment or nerve compression. **Arterial occlusion or dissection** — a cold, pulseless, painful limb, which is a vascular emergency (see 0.6). **Infection.** **Femoral nerve compression** by haematoma, causing thigh weakness and sensory loss.
> **Radial access has substantially lower rates of all of these**, which is why it has become the default for coronary intervention.

**S/Smx:** Groin pain, swelling, pulsatile mass, bruit, bruising, and — for retroperitoneal bleeding — back or flank pain with hypotension and no local signs.

**Ix:** **Duplex ultrasound of the groin** (*why:* the diagnostic test for pseudoaneurysm and AV fistula, distinguishing them from simple haematoma, and it guides thrombin injection; *what:* to-and-fro neck flow, yin-yang sac, fistulous connection). **CT abdomen and pelvis with contrast where hypotension or unexplained haemoglobin fall** (*why:* retroperitoneal haematoma is invisible clinically and this is the only way to find it; *what:* retroperitoneal collection, active extravasation). **Serial FBC** (*why:* a falling haemoglobin is often the only objective sign; *what:* declining haemoglobin). Coagulation profile and review of antiplatelet and anticoagulant therapy (*why:* these patients are almost always on dual antiplatelet therapy plus a periprocedural anticoagulant, which both causes and complicates the bleeding; *what:* INR, agents given). Distal neurovascular examination (*why:* limb ischaemia and femoral nerve compression are limb- and function-threatening; *what:* pulses, capillary refill, sensation, quadriceps power). Group and hold or crossmatch (*why:* transfusion may be required; *what:* units available).

### 0.4.1 Mx – Immediate
Direct pressure. Resuscitate. **Stop or reverse anticoagulation where bleeding is significant** — cross-refer [[A9]] 0.4. **Contact the interventional team that performed the procedure**, who know the access site, the sheath size and the closure device used.

### 0.4.2 Mx – Definitive
Thrombin injection or surgical repair for pseudoaneurysm. Interventional radiology or surgery for ongoing retroperitoneal bleeding. Vascular surgery for occlusion.

### 0.4.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
Document the complication and the access site. Consider radial access for future procedures. Resume antiplatelet therapy after a deliberate risk-benefit decision — **a patient with a recent coronary stent who has antiplatelet therapy stopped is at risk of stent thrombosis**, so the decision needs cardiology input rather than being made unilaterally.

---

## 0.5 Claudication and Peripheral Arterial Disease

**D:** Reproducible ischaemic muscle pain brought on by walking and relieved by rest, caused by arterial stenosis limiting exercise-induced increases in flow.

**A/P:** Atherosclerotic stenosis permits adequate resting flow but prevents the increase required by exercising muscle → anaerobic metabolism and metabolite accumulation in the muscle group **distal to** the lesion → cramping pain → relieved when demand falls. **The site of pain therefore indicates the level of disease**, which makes the history genuinely localising.

> [!tip] The site tells you the level
> **Buttock and hip** → aortoiliac disease. With **erectile dysfunction and absent femoral pulses this is Leriche syndrome.**
> **Thigh** → common femoral or aortoiliac.
> **Calf** — the commonest — → superficial femoral or popliteal.
> **Foot** → tibial or peroneal, characteristic of diabetes.

> [!warning] Vascular versus neurogenic claudication
> Spinal canal stenosis produces "pseudoclaudication" that is frequently mistaken for vascular disease.
> | | Vascular | Neurogenic (spinal stenosis) |
> |---|---|---|
> | Onset distance | **Consistent and reproducible** | Variable day to day |
> | Relief | **Standing still is enough** | **Requires sitting or flexing forward** |
> | Walking uphill | Worse | Often **better** (spine flexed) |
> | Cycling | Worse | Often tolerated well (spine flexed) |
> | Pulses | Reduced or absent | Normal |
> | Sensory symptoms | Uncommon | Common — numbness, tingling |
> The "shopping trolley sign" — relief when leaning forward on a trolley — points strongly to spinal stenosis. Cross-refer [[L4]] Back Pain.

> [!danger] PAD is a marker of systemic atherosclerosis, and the leg is not the main threat
> **A patient with claudication has a substantially elevated risk of myocardial infarction and stroke**, and their cardiovascular mortality far exceeds their risk of limb loss. **The most important interventions are therefore systemic**: smoking cessation, antiplatelet therapy, high-intensity statin, blood pressure and diabetes control.
> Treating the leg while ignoring the coronary and cerebral circulations is treating the least dangerous manifestation.

> [!info] Exercise therapy is first-line and works
> **Supervised exercise programs improve walking distance comparably to revascularisation** for intermittent claudication, without procedural risk. They are consistently under-referred in favour of imaging and intervention. The instruction is to walk to near-maximal claudication pain, rest, and repeat, regularly.

**S/Smx:** Cramping pain in a muscle group on walking, relieved by rest within minutes, reproducible at a consistent distance. Examination: absent or reduced pulses, bruits, cool and hairless skin, thin shiny skin, delayed capillary refill, and dependent rubor with elevation pallor.

**Ix:** **Ankle-brachial index** (*why:* the simple bedside test that confirms and quantifies arterial disease, and it can be done with a hand-held Doppler in clinic; *what:* reduced index — `UNVERIFIED — the diagnostic and severity thresholds`). **Toe-brachial index or toe pressures where the ABI is falsely elevated** (*why:* **medial arterial calcification in diabetes and chronic kidney disease makes the ankle vessels incompressible, producing a normal or high ABI in a patient with severe disease** — this is a critical pitfall and diabetics are exactly the population most affected; *what:* reduced toe pressure). Exercise ABI (*why:* unmasks disease where the resting index is normal but symptoms are typical; *what:* fall in index after exercise). Duplex ultrasound (*why:* localises and grades the stenosis without contrast; *what:* stenosis site and severity). CT or MR angiography (*why:* defines anatomy for planning revascularisation; *what:* lesion location, length, run-off vessels). Fasting lipids, HbA1c, UEC, ECG (*why:* PAD mandates full cardiovascular risk assessment and management, which is where the mortality benefit lies; *what:* risk factors). Lumbar spine imaging where neurogenic claudication is suspected (*why:* the alternative diagnosis; *what:* canal stenosis).

### 0.5.1 Mx – Immediate
Not usually required unless rest pain or acute ischaemia — see 0.6.

### 0.5.2 Mx – Definitive
**Smoking cessation, supervised exercise therapy, antiplatelet, statin, and control of blood pressure and diabetes.** Revascularisation — angioplasty, stenting or bypass — for lifestyle-limiting symptoms failing conservative management, or for limb-threatening ischaemia.

### 0.5.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
Ongoing cardiovascular risk management, foot care education, and annual assessment. **Foot care advice is essential in diabetes** — well-fitting footwear, daily inspection, podiatry, and prompt presentation with any wound.

---

## 0.6 Rest Pain, Chronic Limb-Threatening Ischaemia and Acute Limb Ischaemia

**D:** **Rest pain** is ischaemic pain at rest, indicating perfusion inadequate for basal metabolic requirements. With tissue loss or gangrene it constitutes **chronic limb-threatening ischaemia. Acute limb ischaemia** is a sudden reduction in limb perfusion threatening viability.

> [!tip] Rest pain has a characteristic and diagnostic history
> **Pain in the forefoot and toes, worse at night and when lying flat, relieved by hanging the leg out of bed or sleeping in a chair.**
> The mechanism is straightforward: lying flat removes the gravitational assistance to perfusion of a critically ischaemic foot, and hanging the leg down restores it. A patient describing exactly this has critical ischaemia and needs **urgent** vascular referral, not an outpatient appointment in six weeks.

> [!danger] Acute limb ischaemia — the six Ps, and which two matter most
> **Pain · Pallor · Pulselessness · Perishing cold · Paraesthesia · Paralysis.**
> **Paraesthesia and paralysis are the critical two.** The first four indicate ischaemia; **sensory and motor loss indicate that the nerves and muscle are dying and the limb is immediately threatened.** A patient with a cold pulseless but neurologically intact limb has time; one with sensory loss or weakness does not.
> **This is time-critical — muscle tolerates ischaemia for only a few hours.** Immediate vascular surgical involvement, and immediate anticoagulation while arranging it. `UNVERIFIED — the ischaemic time window, the Rutherford classification categories, and heparin dosing require verification.`

> [!info] Embolic versus thrombotic acute ischaemia
> | | Embolic | Thrombotic (on existing disease) |
> |---|---|---|
> | Onset | **Sudden, dramatic** | More gradual, may be less severe |
> | Prior claudication | **Absent** | Usually present |
> | Contralateral pulses | **Normal** | Also abnormal |
> | Source | **AF**, mural thrombus post-MI, aneurysm, valve | Existing atherosclerosis |
> | Treatment | Embolectomy | Bypass, thrombolysis, angioplasty |
> **A cold pulseless leg in a patient with atrial fibrillation and no history of claudication is embolic** — and this is where the history and the contralateral pulse examination do most of the diagnostic work.

> [!warning] Reperfusion injury
> Restoring flow to an ischaemic limb releases potassium, myoglobin and acid into the circulation, and causes compartment syndrome from muscle oedema — **exactly the crush syndrome physiology in [[A7]] 0.6.** Anticipate hyperkalaemia, arrhythmia, acute kidney injury and the need for fasciotomy. This is why revascularisation of a long-ischaemic limb is a decision made with the whole patient in view.

> [!danger] The diabetic foot is different
> **Neuropathy means the ulcer and the ischaemia may be painless**, so the classic history of rest pain is absent and the patient presents late with established tissue loss and infection. The triad is **neuropathy, ischaemia and infection**, and any diabetic foot ulcer warrants assessment of all three plus imaging for osteomyelitis. Palpable pulses do not exclude significant disease because of the calcification problem in 0.5.

**S/Smx:** Rest pain as described, non-healing ulceration, gangrene, dependent rubor, elevation pallor, absent pulses, and in acute ischaemia the six Ps.

**Ix:** **Urgent vascular surgical assessment — clinical, and it precedes imaging in acute ischaemia** (*why:* the limb is lost by delay, and a threatened limb with sensory or motor loss goes to theatre rather than to the CT scanner; *what:* viability category). ABI and toe pressures (*why:* quantifies perfusion, with the diabetic calcification caveat; *what:* reduced values). CT or MR angiography (*why:* defines anatomy for revascularisation planning, where time permits; *what:* occlusion level, run-off). ECG (*why:* **atrial fibrillation as an embolic source**, and it changes both the diagnosis and the long-term management; *what:* AF). Echocardiography (*why:* mural thrombus, valvular vegetation, or a cardiac source of embolus; *what:* thrombus, vegetation). **CK, UEC and potassium** (*why:* rhabdomyolysis and hyperkalaemia before and especially after reperfusion; *what:* raised CK, potassium). FBC, coagulation, group and hold (*why:* preoperative preparation and thrombophilia consideration in a young patient without atherosclerosis; *what:* baseline). Imaging for osteomyelitis in diabetic foot ulceration (*why:* determines antibiotic duration and the need for surgery, and probe-to-bone is a useful bedside test; *what:* bone involvement on MRI).

### 0.6.1 Mx – Immediate
**Acute limb ischaemia: immediate vascular surgical referral, anticoagulation, analgesia, nil by mouth, keep the limb dependent and warm but do not apply direct heat**, and resuscitate. **Do not wait for imaging in a limb with sensory or motor loss.**
**Rest pain without acute ischaemia:** urgent — not routine — vascular referral, analgesia, and cardiovascular risk management.

### 0.6.2 Mx – Definitive
Embolectomy, thrombolysis, angioplasty or bypass. Amputation where the limb is unsalvageable. Fasciotomy for reperfusion compartment syndrome. **Treat the embolic source** — anticoagulation for AF.

### 0.6.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
Long-term anticoagulation where a cardiac embolic source is found. Full cardiovascular secondary prevention. **Amputation rehabilitation, prosthetics, and psychological support** where limb loss occurs. Diabetic foot surveillance with podiatry, since the contralateral limb is at high risk and the strongest predictor of amputation is a previous one.

---

> Chest pain and aortic dissection → [[B1]] 0.1 · Cardiogenic shock and mechanical complications of MI → [[F0.3]] 0.3 · Acute pulmonary oedema → [[F0.5]] 0.3 · AF and anticoagulation → [[B3]] 0.4 · Rhabdomyolysis, compartment syndrome and reperfusion → [[A7]] 0.6 · Anticoagulant reversal and major haemorrhage → [[A9]] · Rheumatic heart disease and ARF → [[F0.5]] 0.10 · Spinal stenosis and neurogenic claudication → [[L4]] · Pre-eclampsia → [[O2]] · Diabetes and foot care → [[I2]]
