> [!warning] Sourcing
> Written from model knowledge, not retrieved from guidelines. Mechanism, differential structure and discriminators are reliable at intern level. **Every threshold, score component, reference range and dose carries an `UNVERIFIED` marker naming what to check, or has been omitted with the omission stated in place.** Verify Wells scores and D-dimer thresholds, PMR and fibromyalgia criteria, and sarcoma referral pathways against current sources and your local health network's referral guidelines.

---

## 0.1 Oedema — Mechanism and Framework

**D:** Palpable swelling from expansion of the interstitial fluid volume.

**A/P:** Fluid movement across the capillary wall is governed by the **Starling forces**. Oedema arises when one of four things happens:
**1. Increased capillary hydrostatic pressure** — venous obstruction, heart failure, sodium and water retention.
**2. Reduced plasma oncotic pressure** — hypoalbuminaemia from nephrotic syndrome, liver disease, malnutrition or protein-losing enteropathy.
**3. Increased capillary permeability** — inflammation, sepsis, burns, angioedema.
**4. Impaired lymphatic drainage** — lymphoedema from surgery, radiotherapy, malignancy, infection or congenital causes.
Identifying which of the four is operating usually identifies the diagnosis.

> [!tip] The three questions that sort almost every oedema
> **1. Bilateral or unilateral?** Unilateral points to a local cause — venous, lymphatic, infective or traumatic. Bilateral points to a systemic one.
> **2. Pitting or non-pitting?** **Pitting** indicates mobile interstitial fluid — cardiac, renal, hepatic, venous, drug-related. **Non-pitting** suggests **lymphoedema** (protein-rich, fibrotic) or **myxoedema** (mucopolysaccharide deposition in hypothyroidism).
> **3. What is the JVP doing?** **Raised JVP with oedema** → cardiac, pericardial, pulmonary hypertension, or volume overload. **Normal or low JVP with oedema** → hypoalbuminaemia, venous insufficiency, lymphatic obstruction, or drug-induced. This single observation splits the systemic causes efficiently and is free.

> [!warning] Look at the sacrum in a bedbound patient
> Oedema is **dependent**, so it accumulates wherever gravity takes it. In an ambulant patient that is the ankles; **in a bedbound patient it is the sacrum and the posterior thighs**, and examining only the legs will underestimate or entirely miss it. Roll the patient.

**S/Smx:** Swelling, tightness, heaviness, skin changes, and the associated features of the underlying cause. Ask about the timing — worse at the end of the day suggests venous or dependent; worse on waking with periorbital involvement suggests low oncotic pressure.

**Ix:** UEC with eGFR and **urine albumin-creatinine ratio or protein-creatinine ratio** (*why:* nephrotic-range proteinuria is a specific and easily identified cause that requires a nephrology pathway, and it is missed if urine is not tested; *what:* heavy proteinuria, renal impairment). **Serum albumin** (*why:* determines whether reduced oncotic pressure is contributing; *what:* hypoalbuminaemia). LFT (*why:* hepatic synthetic failure; *what:* low albumin, deranged synthetic function). **BNP or NT-proBNP and echocardiography** where cardiac cause is possible (*why:* separates cardiac from non-cardiac oedema, which the examination alone often cannot; *what:* elevation, ventricular function). TFTs (*why:* hypothyroid myxoedema causes non-pitting oedema; *what:* raised TSH). FBC (*why:* anaemia contributes to heart failure and to fatigue; *what:* haemoglobin). **Full medication review** (*why:* drug-induced oedema is common, entirely reversible, and repeatedly investigated as though it were organ failure — see 0.2; *what:* culprit agent).

### 0.1.1 Mx – Immediate
Only where there is airway involvement (angioedema), acute limb ischaemia, or decompensated heart failure.

### 0.1.2 Mx – Definitive
Cause-specific, as below.

### 0.1.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
Compression, elevation, skin care and treatment of the underlying condition.

---

## 0.2 Bilateral and Generalised Oedema

**D:** Symmetrical oedema, usually dependent, from a systemic cause.

> [!danger] Check the medication list before investigating organ failure
> **Dihydropyridine calcium channel blockers — amlodipine, felodipine, nifedipine — cause dose-dependent ankle oedema in a substantial proportion of patients.** The mechanism is preferential arteriolar dilatation raising capillary hydrostatic pressure, **not fluid retention** — which is why **diuretics do not help and simply cause volume depletion.** The treatment is dose reduction or a change of agent.
> This is one of the most commonly missed causes of bilateral leg swelling, and patients are frequently worked up for heart and renal failure while continuing the drug that is causing it.
> Other culprits: **NSAIDs · corticosteroids · pioglitazone · gabapentin and pregabalin · oestrogens and testosterone · minoxidil.**

> [!tip] The main systemic causes and their giveaways
> **Heart failure** — raised JVP, orthopnoea, PND, crackles, third heart sound, raised BNP.
> **Nephrotic syndrome** — **periorbital oedema on waking**, frothy urine, heavy proteinuria, hypoalbuminaemia, hyperlipidaemia. Investigate promptly; it carries thrombotic and infective risk.
> **Chronic liver disease** — ascites disproportionate to leg oedema, stigmata of chronic liver disease, low albumin.
> **Chronic venous insufficiency** — the commonest cause of chronic bilateral leg oedema. **Haemosiderin staining, lipodermatosclerosis ("inverted champagne bottle" leg), varicosities, and ulceration at the medial malleolus.** Worse at the end of the day, better on elevation overnight.
> **Dependency and immobility** — a genuine cause in people who sit all day, and it is under-recognised.
> **Pulmonary hypertension and cor pulmonale** — raised JVP with clear lungs, loud P2.
> **Constrictive pericarditis** — raised JVP with Kussmaul's sign, and it is easy to miss.
> **Hypothyroidism** — non-pitting.
> **Obesity and obstructive sleep apnoea** — frequently combined with several of the above.

> [!warning] Not all bilateral leg swelling with redness is cellulitis
> **Bilateral simultaneous cellulitis is rare.** Bilateral red, warm, swollen lower legs are far more often **venous stasis dermatitis or lipodermatosclerosis**, which are inflammatory but not infective and do not respond to antibiotics. Misdiagnosis leads to repeated antibiotic courses, admissions and resistance while the venous disease goes untreated.
> **If both legs look "cellulitic", question the diagnosis.**

**S/Smx:** Symmetrical pitting swelling, worse dependently, with the associated features above.

**Ix:** As per 0.1. Additionally, in suspected nephrotic syndrome: **lipids and screening for secondary causes** (*why:* hyperlipidaemia is part of the syndrome and diabetes, amyloid, myeloma, lupus and infections are treatable underlying causes; *what:* lipid profile, glucose, autoimmune and paraprotein screen). Venous duplex where venous insufficiency is suspected and intervention is contemplated (*why:* identifies reflux and obstruction amenable to treatment; *what:* reflux, obstruction). Sleep study where OSA is likely (*why:* untreated OSA drives pulmonary hypertension and right heart failure; *what:* apnoea-hypopnoea index).

### 0.2.1 Mx – Immediate
Rarely required.

### 0.2.2 Mx – Definitive
Treat the cause. **Stop or reduce the offending drug.** Diuretics where there is genuine volume overload — **and not where the cause is a calcium channel blocker, lymphoedema or venous insufficiency**, where they do not work and cause harm.

### 0.2.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
**Graduated compression stockings for venous insufficiency, after excluding significant arterial disease** — applying compression to an ischaemic limb causes necrosis, so **check the ABI or the pulses first.** Elevation, exercise, skin care and emollients. Weight management.

---

## 0.3 Unilateral Limb Swelling

**D:** Asymmetrical swelling of one limb, which shifts the differential toward a local cause.

> [!danger] Deep vein thrombosis until proven otherwise
> **Unilateral leg swelling is DVT until excluded**, and the classical features — calf tenderness, warmth, erythema, dilated superficial veins, a measured calf circumference difference — are individually unreliable, which is why structured risk assessment exists.
> Use a validated risk score, then D-dimer in low-probability patients and **compression ultrasound** in the rest. **A negative D-dimer in a high-probability patient does not exclude DVT** and should not be used that way. `UNVERIFIED — Wells score components and thresholds, age-adjusted D-dimer, and the scanning protocol including whether a repeat scan is required.`

> [!warning] Phlegmasia cerulea dolens
> Massive iliofemoral thrombosis causing a **grossly swollen, painful, cyanotic limb with compromised arterial inflow.** It threatens the limb and carries a risk of venous gangrene. It is a vascular emergency requiring urgent thrombolysis or thrombectomy, not routine anticoagulation alone.

> [!tip] The rest of the differential
> **Cellulitis** — fever, spreading erythema with a definable edge, portal of entry. **It can coexist with DVT**, and each is a risk factor for the other.
> **Ruptured Baker's cyst** — "pseudothrombophlebitis", with sudden calf pain and swelling in a patient with knee arthritis, sometimes with a crescent bruise below the malleolus.
> **Compartment syndrome** — pain out of proportion, pain on passive stretch. Cross-refer [[A7]] 0.6.
> **Lymphoedema** — non-pitting, chronic, with skin thickening and **a positive Stemmer sign** (inability to pinch a fold of skin at the base of the second toe). Post-surgical, post-radiotherapy, post-infective, or malignant.
> **Post-thrombotic syndrome** — chronic swelling, pain and skin change after a previous DVT.
> **Pelvic or abdominal malignancy** obstructing venous or lymphatic return — consider particularly in unilateral swelling without another explanation.
> **May-Thurner syndrome** — left iliac vein compression by the right iliac artery, causing left-sided DVT in young patients.
> **Trauma, haematoma, and muscle tear.**

**S/Smx:** Swelling with a measurable circumference difference, pain, warmth, erythema, dilated superficial veins, and the specific features above.

**Ix:** Structured DVT risk assessment (*why:* determines whether D-dimer or imaging is the correct next test, and applying it is more reliable than clinical impression; *what:* probability category). **D-dimer in low-probability patients only** (*why:* useful for its negative predictive value in that group and uninterpretable in high-probability patients, the elderly, pregnancy, malignancy and post-operative states; *what:* negative result excluding DVT in low-risk patients). **Compression ultrasound** (*why:* the diagnostic test; *what:* non-compressible vein, thrombus). CT venography or MR venography for suspected iliac or pelvic thrombus (*why:* ultrasound is unreliable above the inguinal ligament and pelvic thrombus is missed; *what:* iliac thrombus, extrinsic compression). FBC, UEC, coagulation profile (*why:* baseline before anticoagulation and renal function for DOAC dosing; *what:* platelets, renal function). Assessment for occult malignancy in **unprovoked** thrombosis (*why:* unprovoked VTE has a meaningful yield for underlying cancer, and age-appropriate screening plus a careful history and examination is the accepted approach rather than indiscriminate whole-body imaging; *what:* symptoms, examination, up-to-date screening). Thrombophilia testing only in selected cases and **not during the acute event or while on anticoagulation** (*why:* results are unreliable acutely and rarely change management; *what:* deferred).

### 0.3.1 Mx – Immediate
Anticoagulation for confirmed or strongly suspected DVT while awaiting imaging where the delay is significant. Urgent vascular involvement for phlegmasia. Antibiotics for cellulitis. Analgesia and elevation.

### 0.3.2 Mx – Definitive
Anticoagulation for a duration determined by whether the event was provoked or unprovoked. Compression for post-thrombotic syndrome. Complex decongestive therapy for lymphoedema.

### 0.3.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
Cross-refer [[J3]] Bleeding & Thrombosis. **Lymphoedema requires lifelong skin care, compression and prompt treatment of cellulitis**, and referral to a lymphoedema service — it is a chronic condition that is frequently left unmanaged after the initial cause is addressed.

---

## 0.4 Eyelid and Facial Swelling

**D:** Swelling of the periorbital tissues or face, which has a differential distinct from limb oedema because of the loose tissue planes and the anatomy behind them.

> [!danger] Orbital versus preseptal cellulitis — the distinction that saves sight
> **Preseptal (periorbital)** cellulitis is anterior to the orbital septum: eyelid swelling and erythema, **with normal eye movements, normal visual acuity, no proptosis and no pain on eye movement.** Usually managed with oral or intravenous antibiotics.
> **Orbital (post-septal)** cellulitis involves the orbit itself: **proptosis · pain on eye movement · restricted or painful ophthalmoplegia · reduced visual acuity · relative afferent pupillary defect · chemosis · systemic toxicity.**
> **Orbital cellulitis is a sight- and life-threatening emergency** — it causes optic nerve compression, abscess, cavernous sinus thrombosis and intracranial spread. It requires **urgent CT orbits and sinuses, intravenous antibiotics, and ophthalmology and ENT involvement**, and it usually arises from ethmoid sinusitis.
> **Test eye movements and visual acuity in every swollen eyelid.** That examination is the entire distinction, and it takes a minute.

> [!warning] Angioedema
> Non-pitting, asymmetric swelling of lips, tongue, face or larynx. **Histamine-mediated** forms accompany urticaria and respond to adrenaline and antihistamines. **Bradykinin-mediated** forms — ACE inhibitor-induced and hereditary angioedema — have **no urticaria** and do not respond well to the anaphylaxis drugs. Cross-refer [[F0.1]] 0.9 and [[A2]] 0.2. **Assess the airway first in any facial or tongue swelling.**

> [!tip] Superior vena cava obstruction
> **Facial, neck and bilateral upper limb swelling with plethora, distended non-pulsatile neck and chest wall veins, worse on lying flat or bending forward.** **Pemberton's sign** — facial plethora and distress on raising both arms above the head — supports it.
> Usually caused by malignancy, most often lung cancer or lymphoma, and increasingly by intravascular devices and pacing leads. It requires urgent imaging and oncological or interventional management. **A patient with new facial swelling and dilated chest wall veins needs a CT chest, not a course of antihistamines.**

> [!tip] The rest of the differential
> **Nephrotic syndrome** — **periorbital oedema worst on waking**, because overnight recumbency removes gravitational drainage from the loose periorbital tissue. Check the urine.
> **Hypothyroid myxoedema** — non-pitting, with coarse features and other hypothyroid signs.
> **Cushing syndrome** — moon facies, with the other cushingoid features.
> **Dermatomyositis** — **heliotrope periorbital rash with oedema**, Gottron papules and proximal weakness. Worth knowing because it is a paraneoplastic marker.
> **Dental and sinus infection**, and **contact dermatitis** from cosmetics or eye drops.

**S/Smx:** Swelling, erythema, pain, visual change, and the systemic features of the underlying cause.

**Ix:** **Visual acuity, eye movements and pupillary responses** (*why:* the examination that separates preseptal from orbital cellulitis, which determines whether this is an outpatient or an emergency; *what:* reduced acuity, painful or restricted movement, relative afferent pupillary defect). CT orbits and sinuses with contrast where orbital involvement is suspected (*why:* identifies orbital and subperiosteal abscess requiring drainage; *what:* post-septal inflammation, abscess, sinus disease). Urinalysis with protein quantification and albumin (*why:* nephrotic syndrome is a common and easily confirmed cause of periorbital oedema; *what:* heavy proteinuria, hypoalbuminaemia). CT chest where SVC obstruction is suspected (*why:* identifies the mass or thrombus and stages the malignancy; *what:* mediastinal mass, SVC compression or thrombus). TFTs (*why:* myxoedema; *what:* raised TSH). C1 esterase inhibitor level and function where recurrent angioedema without urticaria (*why:* hereditary angioedema requires entirely different treatment; *what:* low level or function). CK and autoimmune screen where dermatomyositis is suspected (*why:* confirms myositis and prompts malignancy screening; *what:* raised CK, myositis antibodies).

### 0.4.1 Mx – Immediate
**Airway assessment in facial or tongue swelling.** Urgent ophthalmology and intravenous antibiotics for orbital cellulitis. Adrenaline for anaphylaxis.

### 0.4.2 Mx – Definitive
Cause-specific. Drainage of orbital abscess. Oncological management and stenting for SVC obstruction.

### 0.4.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
Cessation and permanent documentation of an ACE inhibitor causing angioedema. Nephrology follow-up in nephrotic syndrome.

---

## 0.5 Fatigue, Lethargy and Malaise

**D:** A subjective sense of exhaustion, low energy or being unwell, disproportionate to activity and not relieved by rest.

> [!warning] Two opposite errors, and both are common
> **Over-investigation** — an indiscriminate battery of tests in a patient with an obvious psychosocial cause generates false positives, anxiety and further testing.
> **Dismissal** — attributing fatigue to stress or depression without a basic assessment misses anaemia, hypothyroidism, diabetes, malignancy, coeliac disease and sleep apnoea, all of which are common and treatable.
> The middle path is a **focused history, a proper examination, and a defined baseline panel**, with further testing directed by findings.

> [!danger] Red flags
> **Unintentional weight loss · fever or night sweats · lymphadenopathy · a short history in an older person · focal symptoms or signs · bone pain · new breathlessness · bruising or bleeding · a change from a previously well baseline over weeks rather than years.**

> [!tip] Categories worth working through
> **Haematological** — anaemia of any cause, and **iron deficiency without anaemia also causes fatigue**, so check ferritin rather than haemoglobin alone.
> **Endocrine** — hypothyroidism, diabetes, adrenal insufficiency, hypercalcaemia.
> **Infective** — chronic viral infection including hepatitis and HIV, tuberculosis, infective endocarditis, and post-viral states.
> **Inflammatory** — connective tissue disease, inflammatory bowel disease, polymyalgia rheumatica.
> **Malignancy** — particularly haematological.
> **Cardiorespiratory** — heart failure, chronic lung disease.
> **Sleep** — **obstructive sleep apnoea is very commonly the answer and very commonly not asked about.** Ask about snoring, witnessed apnoeas, and daytime sleepiness as distinct from fatigue.
> **Drugs** — beta-blockers, antihistamines, opioids, antidepressants, statins, and alcohol.
> **Psychiatric** — **depression is the single commonest treatable cause**, and it must be asked about directly rather than inferred from a normal blood panel. Cross-refer [[N4]].
> **Coeliac disease** — frequently presents as fatigue with minimal gastrointestinal symptoms.

> [!info] Chronic fatigue and post-viral states
> Persistent debilitating fatigue with post-exertional worsening, unrefreshing sleep and cognitive difficulty, following an infection or arising insidiously, is a recognised clinical entity variously labelled ME/CFS or post-viral fatigue.
> **It is a real condition and dismissing it damages both the patient and the therapeutic relationship.** It is also a diagnosis made positively, after reasonable exclusion of alternatives, rather than a label for unexplained symptoms.
> **Management guidance has shifted substantially**, moving away from graded exercise as a universal prescription and toward **activity management and pacing that respects post-exertional symptom exacerbation**, alongside symptom-directed treatment and management of comorbid sleep and mood problems. `UNVERIFIED — the current recommended approach, and the status of graded exercise therapy, require verification against current guidance, as this area has changed and remains contested.`

**S/Smx:** Duration, pattern through the day, relation to exertion, sleep quality, mood, appetite, weight change, and the red flags above.

**Ix:** A **defined baseline panel** rather than an open-ended one — **FBC, UEC, LFT, CRP and ESR, TSH, HbA1c or fasting glucose, ferritin, calcium, and coeliac serology** (*why:* this set covers the great majority of organic causes at reasonable cost, and each abnormal result has a clear next step; *what:* anaemia, renal or hepatic disease, inflammation, thyroid dysfunction, diabetes, iron deficiency, hypercalcaemia, coeliac disease). Vitamin D and B12 where risk factors exist (*why:* deficiency is common in specific groups and correctable; *what:* deficiency). CK where muscle symptoms (*why:* myositis and statin myopathy; *what:* elevation). **Direct enquiry about mood, sleep and alcohol** (*why:* these are the commonest causes and are found by asking, not by testing; *what:* depression, OSA, alcohol use). Sleep study where OSA features are present (*why:* highly treatable and commonly missed; *what:* apnoea-hypopnoea index). HIV and hepatitis serology where risk factors (*why:* treatable chronic infection; *what:* positive serology). Chest radiograph and further imaging only where red flags direct it (*why:* untargeted imaging in fatigue has low yield and high incidentaloma rate; *what:* directed findings).

### 0.5.1 Mx – Immediate
Rarely required.

### 0.5.2 Mx – Definitive
Treat the identified cause. Where none is found, **say so honestly rather than continuing to test**, and offer a structured follow-up plan with a review interval — this is more reassuring than open-ended investigation.

### 0.5.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
Sleep hygiene, graded and paced activity where appropriate, treatment of mood disorder, and continuity of care with one clinician, which matters more in this presentation than in almost any other.

---

## 0.6 Generalised Weakness

**D:** Reduced muscle power, which must first be distinguished from fatigue.

> [!danger] "Weak" means at least three different things
> **Ask the patient what they cannot do.**
> **· True weakness** — reduced power. "I can't lift my arm above my head", "I can't get out of a chair without using my hands." This is a neurological or muscular problem and requires localisation.
> **· Fatigue or asthenia** — power is normal but effort feels excessive. This is 0.5.
> **· Functional limitation** — pain, joint restriction or breathlessness limiting activity, with normal power.
> Conflating these sends the workup in entirely the wrong direction.

> [!tip] Localise true weakness
> **Upper motor neurone** — increased tone, brisk reflexes, upgoing plantars, pyramidal distribution.
> **Lower motor neurone** — wasting, fasciculation, reduced tone and reflexes.
> **Neuromuscular junction** — **fatigable** weakness, ptosis, diplopia, bulbar symptoms; worse through the day.
> **Muscle** — **symmetrical proximal** weakness, normal sensation, preserved reflexes until late. Difficulty rising from a chair, climbing stairs, or raising the arms to comb hair.
> Cross-refer [[D4]] for the full neurological approach.

> [!warning] Acute or rapidly progressive weakness is an emergency
> **Guillain–Barré syndrome and myasthenic crisis** both present as ascending or fatigable weakness that can progress to respiratory failure over hours to days. **Monitor forced vital capacity, not oxygen saturation** — cross-refer [[F0.5]] 0.5. Do not send a patient with new progressive weakness home to be seen in clinic.

> [!tip] The treatable systemic causes of proximal weakness
> **Electrolytes** — hypokalaemia, hyperkalaemia, hypercalcaemia, hypomagnesaemia, hypophosphataemia.
> **Endocrine** — thyroid disease in both directions, **Cushing syndrome and exogenous corticosteroids (steroid myopathy)**, adrenal insufficiency, **osteomalacia from vitamin D deficiency**, hyperparathyroidism.
> **Drugs** — **statins**, colchicine, corticosteroids, and alcohol.
> **Inflammatory myopathy** — polymyositis and dermatomyositis, with a raised CK and, in dermatomyositis, the characteristic rash and a paraneoplastic association.
> **Deconditioning** — genuine, common after illness or hospitalisation, and improved by rehabilitation rather than by more tests.

**S/Smx:** Distribution and pattern of weakness, its time course, fatigability, associated sensory symptoms, bulbar and respiratory symptoms, and systemic features.

**Ix:** UEC with **potassium, calcium, magnesium and phosphate** (*why:* electrolyte disturbance is a common, rapidly reversible cause of profound weakness; *what:* derangement). **CK** (*why:* separates myopathic from neuropathic causes and identifies statin myopathy and inflammatory myositis; *what:* elevation). TFTs (*why:* both hypo- and hyperthyroidism cause proximal myopathy; *what:* abnormal TSH). Vitamin D, calcium, phosphate and ALP (*why:* osteomalacia causes proximal weakness with bone pain and is fully treatable; *what:* low vitamin D, raised ALP). Morning cortisol or short synacthen where adrenal insufficiency is possible (*why:* treatable and easily missed; *what:* low cortisol). FBC, ESR and CRP (*why:* inflammatory and malignant causes; *what:* raised markers). **Serial forced vital capacity where weakness is acute or progressive** (*why:* the parameter that detects impending respiratory failure in neuromuscular disease, which gases and oximetry will not; *what:* declining FVC). Acetylcholine receptor antibodies, nerve conduction studies and EMG where indicated (*why:* confirm myasthenia and localise the lesion; *what:* antibodies, decrement, denervation). Malignancy screening in confirmed dermatomyositis (*why:* the paraneoplastic association is strong enough to warrant an age-appropriate search; *what:* underlying cancer).

### 0.6.1 Mx – Immediate
Correct electrolytes. **Assess respiratory function in acute or progressive weakness and escalate.** Exclude cord compression where there is a sensory level, bladder or bowel disturbance, or back pain — cross-refer [[D3]] and [[L4]].

### 0.6.2 Mx – Definitive
Cause-specific. Neurology involvement for suspected neuromuscular disease.

### 0.6.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
Physiotherapy and rehabilitation, which is the definitive treatment for deconditioning and an important adjunct in everything else. Medication review.

---

## 0.7 Generalised Pain

**D:** Widespread musculoskeletal pain affecting multiple regions, without a single localising lesion.

> [!info] Polymyalgia rheumatica — the one not to miss
> **Age over 50 — essentially never younger** · **bilateral shoulder and pelvic girdle pain and stiffness** · **prolonged morning stiffness** · raised ESR and CRP · systemic features of fatigue, weight loss and low-grade fever · and a **dramatic response to low-dose corticosteroids**, often within days, which is itself close to diagnostic.
> **Ask specifically about giant cell arteritis symptoms in every PMR patient** — new headache, scalp tenderness, jaw claudication, and visual disturbance. **GCA causes irreversible blindness and requires immediate high-dose steroids on suspicion, before biopsy.** The two conditions overlap substantially and the patient must be told what symptoms to report urgently. Cross-refer [[F0.5]] 0.6.

> [!tip] Fibromyalgia is a positive diagnosis, not a residual label
> Widespread pain for months, with **fatigue, unrefreshing sleep, and cognitive difficulty ("fibro fog")**, often with a history of a triggering physical or psychological stressor. Examination shows widespread tenderness without synovitis, and inflammatory markers are normal.
> **It is diagnosed by its features, not by the absence of anything else**, and telling a patient "all your tests are normal" without a diagnosis is unhelpful and often harmful. Explaining central sensitisation — that the pain is real and reflects amplified pain processing rather than tissue damage — is a genuinely therapeutic intervention.
> **Management is exercise, sleep, psychological therapy and selected medications; opioids are ineffective and harmful.** `UNVERIFIED — diagnostic criteria and recommended pharmacotherapy.`

> [!danger] Red flags in widespread pain
> **Focal bone pain, particularly at night · unexplained weight loss · age over 50 with new onset · raised inflammatory markers · anaemia · hypercalcaemia · renal impairment · a history of malignancy · fever.**
> **The combination of bone pain, anaemia, renal impairment and hypercalcaemia is myeloma until proven otherwise** — the CRAB features — and it is a diagnosis routinely delayed by attributing back and rib pain to degenerative disease. Cross-refer [[J4]].
> **Metastatic bone disease** likewise presents as generalised or focal bone pain, and progressive pain unrelieved by rest with a known primary requires imaging.

> [!tip] The rest of the differential
> **Inflammatory arthritis** with polyarticular involvement · **hypothyroidism** · **vitamin D deficiency and osteomalacia** — bone pain with proximal weakness · **statin-associated muscle symptoms** · **depression**, which both causes and amplifies pain · **hypermobility spectrum disorders** in younger patients · **medication withdrawal**, including opioid-induced hyperalgesia in chronic opioid users.

**S/Smx:** Distribution, duration, diurnal pattern, morning stiffness and its duration, associated systemic features, sleep quality and mood.

**Ix:** **FBC, ESR and CRP** (*why:* separates inflammatory from non-inflammatory causes, which is the primary branch, and PMR and GCA both show markedly raised inflammatory markers; *what:* raised markers, anaemia). **Calcium, phosphate, ALP and vitamin D** (*why:* hypercalcaemia points to myeloma or malignancy, and low vitamin D with raised ALP suggests osteomalacia; *what:* the pattern). UEC (*why:* renal impairment as part of the CRAB features and as a cause of bone disease; *what:* creatinine). **Serum protein electrophoresis and free light chains where myeloma is possible** (*why:* the specific test, and it is not part of a routine panel so it must be asked for; *what:* paraprotein, abnormal light chain ratio). TFTs (*why:* hypothyroidism; *what:* raised TSH). CK (*why:* myositis and statin myopathy; *what:* elevation). Autoimmune serology where inflammatory features are present (*why:* connective tissue disease; *what:* ANA, rheumatoid factor, anti-CCP — interpreted with the caveat that these have poor specificity when ordered without clinical indication). Imaging directed at focal pain (*why:* identifies metastasis, fracture and infection; *what:* lytic or sclerotic lesions, fracture).

### 0.7.1 Mx – Immediate
**High-dose corticosteroids immediately on suspicion of giant cell arteritis**, before temporal artery biopsy. Analgesia otherwise.

### 0.7.2 Mx – Definitive
Cause-specific. Corticosteroids with a slow taper for PMR, with steroid-sparing agents where relapse occurs.

### 0.7.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
**Bone protection for anyone on prolonged corticosteroids** — calcium, vitamin D, bisphosphonate consideration, and monitoring for glucose intolerance. Multidisciplinary management in fibromyalgia with an emphasis on function rather than pain elimination.

---

## 0.8 Undifferentiated Lump

**D:** A palpable mass, of any site, requiring characterisation and a decision about investigation.

> [!tip] What to characterise
> **Site · size · shape · surface · consistency** (soft, firm, rubbery, hard) **· mobility** — mobile, tethered, or fixed to deep structures **· fluctuance · pulsatility and expansility** (an expansile mass is an aneurysm until proven otherwise) **· transillumination · overlying skin change · tenderness · temperature · relation to surrounding structures · duration and rate of growth.**
> **Rate of growth and fixity carry more diagnostic weight than any other features.**

> [!danger] Soft tissue sarcoma — do not biopsy before referral
> Features suggesting sarcoma: **larger than approximately 5 cm · deep to the deep fascia · firm or hard · fixed · increasing in size · painless · or recurrence after a previous "lipoma" excision.**
> **These lesions should be referred to a specialist sarcoma service for imaging and biopsy planning BEFORE any biopsy or excision is attempted.**
> An unplanned excision or a poorly placed biopsy tract contaminates tissue planes, converts a limb-sparing operation into a more extensive one, and worsens outcomes. **"I'll just take it out and send it" is the specific error this guidance exists to prevent.** `UNVERIFIED — the size threshold and referral criteria; obtain your state's sarcoma referral pathway.`

> [!warning] Lymphadenopathy
> **Localised** reactive nodes near a site of infection are common and benign. Concerning features: **size above roughly 1–2 cm persisting beyond a few weeks · hard or rubbery consistency · fixed or matted · painless · progressive · generalised distribution · accompanied by B symptoms — fever, night sweats, unexplained weight loss.**
> **Rubbery, painless, mobile nodes suggest lymphoma. Hard, fixed nodes suggest metastatic carcinoma.**
> **A supraclavicular node is pathological until proven otherwise** — the left one (Virchow's node) classically drains intra-abdominal malignancy. Never dismiss it.
> `UNVERIFIED — size thresholds and the duration beyond which investigation is indicated.`

> [!tip] Common benign lesions and their giveaways
> **Lipoma** — soft, lobulated, mobile, slow-growing, subcutaneous. **Epidermoid or sebaceous cyst** — attached to skin with a **central punctum**. **Ganglion** — near a joint or tendon sheath, transilluminates. **Abscess** — tender, hot, fluctuant, with overlying erythema. **Hernia** — reducible, with a cough impulse, at a hernial orifice. **Aneurysm** — **expansile** pulsation, not merely transmitted.

**S/Smx:** As above, with systemic symptoms — weight loss, fever, night sweats — and a full examination of regional nodes and the likely primary sites.

**Ix:** **Ultrasound as first-line for most superficial lumps** (*why:* distinguishes solid from cystic, assesses vascularity and depth relative to fascia, and is quick, cheap and radiation-free; *what:* cystic versus solid, depth, vascularity, nodal architecture). **MRI for any lesion suspected of being a sarcoma, arranged through the sarcoma service** (*why:* defines extent and relationship to neurovascular structures for surgical planning, and must precede biopsy; *what:* size, depth, compartment involvement). CT chest, abdomen and pelvis where malignancy is suspected (*why:* identifies a primary and stages disease; *what:* primary lesion, metastases). **Core biopsy or excision biopsy of a lymph node rather than fine needle aspiration where lymphoma is suspected** (*why:* lymphoma diagnosis requires assessment of nodal architecture, which cytology from an FNA cannot provide, so an FNA frequently produces a non-diagnostic result and delays diagnosis; *what:* histological subtype). FBC, LDH, film and ESR (*why:* haematological malignancy screening; *what:* cytopenias, blasts, raised LDH). Site-specific investigation — cross-refer [[F4]] Neck Lumps, [[H4]] Groin Lump, [[O7]] Breast Lump, [[G5]] Skin Lumps.

### 0.8.1 Mx – Immediate
Only for abscess (drainage) or a rapidly expanding pulsatile mass (vascular emergency).

### 0.8.2 Mx – Definitive
Site- and diagnosis-specific. **Referral before biopsy where sarcoma is a possibility.**

### 0.8.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
Follow-up of any lesion managed conservatively, with a defined review interval and clear instructions about what change should prompt earlier return.

---

> [!note] Cross-references
> Heart failure and pulmonary oedema → [[F0.5]] 0.3 and [[F0.3]] 0.3 · Nephrotic syndrome and proteinuria → [[H1]] · VTE and anticoagulation → [[J3]] · Angioedema and anaphylaxis → [[F0.1]] 0.9 · Airway involvement in facial swelling → [[A2]] 0.2 · Neuromuscular weakness and FVC monitoring → [[F0.5]] 0.5 and [[D4]] · Giant cell arteritis → [[F0.5]] 0.6 · Myeloma and paraproteins → [[J4]] · Depression → [[N4]] · Site-specific lumps → [[F4]], [[H4]], [[O7]], [[G5]]


<!-- ===== SOURCE: CV-X_Chronic_Heart_Failure.md ===== -->


> [!warning] Sourcing
> Written from model knowledge, not retrieved from guidelines. Pathophysiology, framework and clinical reasoning are reliable at intern level. **Every ejection fraction cut-off, natriuretic peptide threshold, dose, titration target and device criterion carries an `UNVERIFIED` marker naming what to check, or has been omitted with the omission stated in place.**
> **Heart failure pharmacotherapy has changed substantially in recent years** — the four-pillar approach, SGLT2 inhibitors in both HFrEF and HFpEF, and ARNI in place of ACE inhibitors are all relatively recent. Verify against the **National Heart Foundation / CSANZ Guidelines for the Prevention, Detection and Management of Heart Failure in Australia**, eTG Cardiovascular, and PBS authority criteria — which determine what can actually be prescribed here.
>
> Acute pulmonary oedema and cardiogenic shock are built in [[F0.5]] 0.3 and [[F0.3]] 0.3 and are cross-referenced rather than repeated.

---

## 0.1 Definition, Classification and Pathophysiology

**D:** A clinical syndrome of symptoms and signs caused by a structural or functional cardiac abnormality, resulting in **reduced cardiac output and/or elevated intracardiac pressures** at rest or during stress.

> [!tip] Classification by ejection fraction — because it determines the treatment
> **· HFrEF — heart failure with reduced ejection fraction.** The group with the strongest evidence base and the largest treatment effect.
> **· HFmrEF — mildly reduced ejection fraction.** An intermediate category; increasingly treated like HFrEF.
> **· HFpEF — preserved ejection fraction.** Requires objective evidence of diastolic dysfunction or raised filling pressures — a normal EF alone is not enough, and symptoms with a normal echo are not automatically HFpEF.
> `UNVERIFIED — the EF cut-offs defining each category.`
>
> **Also classified by:**
> **· NYHA class I–IV** — current symptom severity, which **fluctuates** and is reassessed at every visit.
> **· ACC/AHA stages A–D** — structural progression, which **only moves in one direction.** A patient who improves from NYHA III to NYHA I remains in the same structural stage.

> [!info] The neurohormonal model — and why it explains the whole treatment strategy
> An index injury reduces cardiac output → the body responds as though to haemorrhage → **activation of the renin-angiotensin-aldosterone system, the sympathetic nervous system, and vasopressin.**
> **These responses are adaptive acutely and maladaptive chronically:** salt and water retention increases preload and congestion; vasoconstriction increases afterload and further reduces output; and chronic catecholamine and aldosterone exposure drives **adverse ventricular remodelling — myocyte hypertrophy, apoptosis and interstitial fibrosis** — which worsens function further, in a self-reinforcing spiral.
>
> **This single concept explains the two most counterintuitive facts in heart failure:**
> **1. Beta-blockers improve survival despite being negative inotropes**, because blocking chronic sympathetic drive prevents remodelling — even though they may transiently worsen symptoms on initiation.
> **2. Inotropes make people feel better and die sooner.** Increasing contractility increases myocardial oxygen demand and arrhythmia without addressing the underlying process.
> **Every disease-modifying drug in heart failure blocks a neurohormonal pathway. Diuretics, which do not, relieve symptoms without improving survival.**

> [!tip] Aetiology — always look for a cause, because several are reversible
> **· Ischaemic heart disease — the commonest cause in Australia.**
> **· Hypertension.**
> **· Valvular disease** — cross-refer [[B5]] 0.1.
> **· Arrhythmia** — **tachycardia-mediated cardiomyopathy from persistent uncontrolled AF or another tachyarrhythmia is REVERSIBLE with rate or rhythm control**, and is missed when the AF is assumed to be a consequence rather than the cause.
> **· Alcohol** — dose-related, and **substantially reversible with abstinence.**
> **· Cardiotoxic drugs** — anthracyclines, trastuzumab, **immune checkpoint inhibitors (myocarditis — cross-refer [[A5]] 0.7)**, and stimulants including methamphetamine, which is an increasingly recognised cause of cardiomyopathy in younger Australians.
> **· Myocarditis** — viral, autoimmune.
> **· Infiltrative** — **cardiac amyloidosis (increasingly recognised, and ATTR amyloidosis is now treatable, which makes diagnosing it worthwhile)**, sarcoidosis, haemochromatosis.
> **· Peripartum cardiomyopathy** — cross-refer [[B5]] 0.3.
> **· Familial dilated cardiomyopathy** — take a family history and consider screening relatives.
> **· Rheumatic heart disease** — with disproportionate burden in Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander communities. Cross-refer [[F0.5]] 0.10 and [[AU1]].
> **· High-output failure** — **severe anaemia, thyrotoxicosis, arteriovenous fistula, thiamine deficiency (beriberi), Paget disease.** Treat the cause, not the heart.

**S/Smx:** Dyspnoea, orthopnoea, paroxysmal nocturnal dyspnoea, fatigue, reduced exercise tolerance, ankle swelling, abdominal distension, early satiety, nocturia, and weight gain. Signs: raised JVP, displaced apex, third heart sound, basal crackles, peripheral or sacral oedema, hepatomegaly, ascites, and a narrow pulse pressure with cool peripheries in low-output states.

**Ix:** See 0.2.

### 0.1.1 Mx – Immediate
Only in decompensation — see 0.5.

### 0.1.2 Mx – Definitive
See 0.3 and 0.4.

### 0.1.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
See 0.7.

---

## 0.2 Diagnosis and Assessment

> [!warning] Individual symptoms and signs perform poorly — the diagnosis needs objective evidence
> **Orthopnoea, PND and a raised JVP are relatively specific but insensitive. Ankle oedema and basal crackles are neither.** Crackles in an older patient are frequently atelectasis or lung disease, and ankle oedema is more often venous, drug-induced or dependent — cross-refer [[B6]] 0.2.
> **The diagnosis of heart failure requires objective cardiac assessment**, and labelling a breathless oedematous patient with "heart failure" without an echocardiogram commits them to lifelong therapy that may be inappropriate.

> [!tip] Natriuretic peptides — use them to rule OUT
> **BNP and NT-proBNP have high negative predictive value.** A low level in an untreated patient makes heart failure unlikely and should redirect the workup.
> **A raised level is far less specific.** It rises with **age, atrial fibrillation, renal impairment, pulmonary embolism, sepsis, right heart strain, and critical illness generally.**
> **And it is LOWERED by obesity**, which is a genuine pitfall — an obese patient with heart failure may have a deceptively low level.
> `UNVERIFIED — the rule-out thresholds, which differ between BNP and NT-proBNP, between acute and non-acute presentations, and by age.`

> [!tip] Echocardiography is the essential test
> It provides: **ejection fraction, chamber dimensions, regional wall motion (suggesting ischaemic aetiology), valvular function, diastolic function and estimated filling pressures, right ventricular function and pulmonary pressures, and pericardial assessment.**
> **A completely normal 12-lead ECG makes HFrEF unlikely** — a useful and cheap negative.

> [!warning] Then investigate the cause — this step is frequently skipped
> Establishing "heart failure" and starting the drugs without determining **why** misses reversible and specifically treatable causes.
> **· Ischaemia** — the commonest cause, and revascularisation may be indicated. Functional testing, CT coronary angiography, or invasive angiography.
> **· Iron deficiency** — **very common in heart failure, and it worsens symptoms and exercise capacity independently of anaemia.** **Intravenous iron improves symptoms and quality of life in iron-deficient HFrEF even when the haemoglobin is normal**, so **ferritin and transferrin saturation should be checked in everyone** — and the thresholds used in heart failure are different from those used for anaemia generally.
> **· Thyroid disease · diabetes · alcohol · sleep apnoea.**
> **· Cardiac MRI** — increasingly central for aetiology, distinguishing ischaemic from non-ischaemic scar and identifying myocarditis, infiltration and amyloid.
> **· Cardiac amyloidosis** — suspect with **left ventricular hypertrophy out of proportion to blood pressure, low ECG voltages despite thick walls (a striking discordance), bilateral carpal tunnel syndrome preceding the cardiac presentation by years, spinal stenosis, and intolerance of standard heart failure medications.** **Bone scintigraphy identifies ATTR amyloidosis non-invasively, and treatment now exists** — which is why this diagnosis has moved from academic to actionable.

**Ix:** **Echocardiography** (*why:* the essential test — it establishes the diagnosis, categorises by EF, and identifies aetiology; *what:* EF, chamber size, wall motion, valves, diastolic parameters, RV function). **BNP or NT-proBNP** (*why:* rule-out value in the undifferentiated breathless patient; *what:* level, interpreted with the caveats above). ECG (*why:* prior infarction, LVH, bundle branch block (which affects device eligibility), AF, and low voltages suggesting amyloid; *what:* the trace). CXR (*why:* congestion, cardiomegaly, and alternative pulmonary causes of breathlessness; *what:* upper lobe diversion, Kerley B lines, effusions). **FBC and iron studies including ferritin and transferrin saturation** (*why:* anaemia and iron deficiency are both common, both worsen symptoms, and iron deficiency is specifically treatable in HF; *what:* anaemia, low ferritin or transferrin saturation). UEC, eGFR and electrolytes (*why:* baseline before RAAS blockade, and cardiorenal interaction; *what:* renal function, potassium, sodium). LFT (*why:* hepatic congestion; *what:* congestive pattern). TFTs (*why:* both hypo- and hyperthyroidism cause or worsen heart failure; *what:* abnormal TSH). HbA1c and lipids (*why:* risk factor management; *what:* glycaemia, lipids). **Assessment for ischaemia** (*why:* the commonest cause and potentially revascularisable; *what:* inducible ischaemia or coronary disease). **Cardiac MRI in non-ischaemic or unexplained cardiomyopathy** (*why:* identifies myocarditis, infiltration, amyloid and scar pattern, which changes management and prognosis; *what:* late gadolinium enhancement pattern). Bone scintigraphy where ATTR amyloid is suspected (*why:* non-invasive diagnosis of a now-treatable condition; *what:* cardiac tracer uptake). Sleep study where indicated (*why:* sleep-disordered breathing is common in HF and treating it improves symptoms; *what:* apnoea-hypopnoea index). Genetic testing and family screening in dilated cardiomyopathy (*why:* relatives may be affected and screenable; *what:* pathogenic variant).

### 0.2.1 Mx – Immediate
Not applicable outside decompensation.

### 0.2.2 Mx – Definitive
See 0.3 and 0.4.

### 0.2.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
Repeat echocardiography after a period of optimal therapy — **ventricular function frequently improves substantially, and this determines device eligibility and prognosis.**

---

## 0.3 HFrEF Management — The Four Pillars

> [!danger] The modern approach: four drug classes, started early, titrated in parallel
> **Each of the four independently reduces mortality, and their benefits are additive.** The older approach — start one, titrate to maximum over months, then add the next — leaves patients unprotected for a long period.
> **Current practice is to initiate all four at low dose as early as tolerated, then uptitrate**, because the mortality benefit appears within weeks and a patient on four low doses does better than one on a single maximal dose.
>
> **1. ARNI (sacubitril/valsartan), or an ACE inhibitor / ARB** if ARNI is not available or tolerated.
> **2. Beta-blocker** — **only the evidence-based agents: bisoprolol, carvedilol, metoprolol extended-release, nebivolol.** **This is a class where the specific agent matters — do not substitute another beta-blocker and assume equivalent benefit.**
> **3. Mineralocorticoid receptor antagonist** — spironolactone or eplerenone.
> **4. SGLT2 inhibitor** — dapagliflozin or empagliflozin. **The benefit is independent of whether the patient has diabetes**, which surprises people, and it is now a core heart failure drug rather than a diabetes drug.
> `UNVERIFIED — all agents, starting doses, target doses, titration intervals and PBS authority criteria.`

> [!warning] Diuretics relieve symptoms and do not prolong life
> **Loop diuretics treat congestion. They have no mortality benefit and they activate the renin-angiotensin system.**
> **Use the lowest dose that maintains euvolaemia**, and **reduce or stop them if the patient becomes dry** — a patient over-diuresed into hypovolaemia will not tolerate the drugs that actually improve survival, and diuretic dose is frequently escalated and never revisited.
> Diuretic dose is titrated to weight and symptoms, ideally with the patient adjusting it themselves under an action plan — see 0.7.

> [!tip] The other agents
> **· Ivabradine** — for patients in sinus rhythm with a persistently elevated heart rate despite a maximally tolerated beta-blocker. It does not work in AF.
> **· Hydralazine plus isosorbide dinitrate** — where RAAS blockade cannot be used, and with specific evidence in some populations.
> **· Digoxin** — reduces hospitalisation, **no mortality benefit**, useful for symptoms and for rate control in AF.
> **· Vericiguat** and other newer agents in selected patients.

> [!danger] Practical prescribing points that cause the most trouble
> **· Expect the creatinine to rise and the potassium to increase when starting RAAS blockade or an MRA.** A modest, stable rise is **expected and acceptable** and reflects the drug working — it is **not** a reason to stop. Stopping reflexively at the first creatinine bump denies the patient the most beneficial drugs they will receive. **Recheck electrolytes and renal function after initiation and after each uptitration**, and know the thresholds at which action is genuinely required. `UNVERIFIED — the acceptable creatinine rise and the potassium thresholds for dose reduction or cessation.`
> **· Start beta-blockers when the patient is euvolaemic, not while congested.** "Start low, go slow." **Warn the patient that symptoms may transiently worsen for a few weeks before improving** — otherwise they stop the drug and conclude it made them worse.
> **· SGLT2 inhibitors cause euglycaemic ketoacidosis**, particularly with intercurrent illness, fasting or surgery — **sick day rules and perioperative withholding must be explained.** Cross-refer [[F0.2]] 0.3.
> **· ARNI must not be co-administered with an ACE inhibitor, and requires a washout period after stopping one**, because of angioedema risk. `UNVERIFIED — the washout interval.`
> **· MRAs cause gynaecomastia (spironolactone more than eplerenone) and hyperkalaemia.**

> [!danger] Drugs to avoid or use with great caution in HFrEF
> **· NSAIDs** — sodium retention, renal impairment, and they antagonise diuretics and RAAS blockade. **A very common cause of decompensation, and frequently bought over the counter without being mentioned.**
> **· Non-dihydropyridine calcium channel blockers (verapamil, diltiazem)** — negatively inotropic and contraindicated in HFrEF. **Dihydropyridines (amlodipine) are haemodynamically neutral and may be used** — this distinction matters and is examined. Cross-refer [[F0.1]] 0.7.
> **· Thiazolidinediones (pioglitazone)** — fluid retention.
> **· Class I antiarrhythmics such as flecainide** — pro-arrhythmic in structural heart disease.
> **· High-dose corticosteroids** — fluid retention.
> **· Some complementary preparations** and high-sodium effervescent formulations, which are an overlooked sodium load.

> [!warning] Cardiac rehabilitation is under-referred
> **Exercise-based cardiac rehabilitation improves symptoms, quality of life and hospitalisation in heart failure**, and it is offered to a fraction of eligible patients. **Refer.**

**Ix:** UEC and potassium before and after each initiation and uptitration (*why:* the expected and the dangerous changes both occur here; *what:* creatinine, potassium). Blood pressure and heart rate at each visit (*why:* determines titration headroom; *what:* the limiting parameter). **Repeat echocardiography after a period of optimised therapy** (*why:* function frequently improves, which changes prognosis and device eligibility — **and a patient whose EF has recovered on treatment should generally continue it, because withdrawal causes relapse**; *what:* EF change). Iron studies (*why:* IV iron improves symptoms; *what:* ferritin, transferrin saturation).

### 0.3.1 Mx – Immediate
See 0.5 for decompensation.

### 0.3.2 Mx – Definitive
Four-pillar therapy, uptitrated to target or maximally tolerated doses, plus device therapy where indicated (0.6).

### 0.3.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
See 0.7.

---

## 0.4 HFpEF Management

**A/P:** Impaired ventricular relaxation and increased passive stiffness → **elevated filling pressures at normal or near-normal volumes** → congestion and exertional dyspnoea with a preserved ejection fraction. Driven by hypertension, obesity, ageing, diabetes, atrial fibrillation and chronic kidney disease, with systemic inflammation and microvascular dysfunction implicated.

> [!tip] The typical patient
> **Older, more often female, with hypertension, obesity, atrial fibrillation, diabetes and chronic kidney disease.** Exertional dyspnoea and fatigue with a preserved EF on echocardiography.
> **The diagnosis requires objective evidence of raised filling pressures or diastolic dysfunction**, not simply symptoms plus a normal EF — because "breathless with a normal echo" has a wide differential including deconditioning, obesity, lung disease, anaemia and ischaemia.

> [!danger] Exclude the mimics before settling on HFpEF
> **· Cardiac amyloidosis** — see 0.2. Increasingly recognised as a substantial minority of what was called HFpEF, and ATTR is treatable.
> **· Constrictive pericarditis** — potentially curable surgically, and distinguishing it from restrictive physiology is a specific echocardiographic and haemodynamic question.
> **· Hypertrophic cardiomyopathy.**
> **· Valvular disease**, particularly severe aortic stenosis and mitral regurgitation.
> **· High-output states** — anaemia, thyrotoxicosis, arteriovenous fistula.
> **Labelling a patient as HFpEF closes the diagnostic process**, so it is worth being deliberate about it.

> [!warning] The treatment position has changed
> **For years the accurate statement was that no therapy improved outcomes in HFpEF. That is no longer true — SGLT2 inhibitors now have evidence of benefit in HFpEF**, and this represents the first substantial change in the field.
> The remainder of management is:
> **· Diuretics for congestion** — symptom relief only, and these patients are **preload-dependent and over-diurese easily**, so titrate carefully.
> **· Aggressive blood pressure control** — the single most important preventive and therapeutic measure.
> **· Rate and rhythm control of atrial fibrillation**, which is poorly tolerated because atrial contribution to filling matters more in a stiff ventricle. **Restoring sinus rhythm can produce marked symptomatic improvement.**
> **· Weight loss and exercise training**, both of which have evidence in HFpEF.
> **· Treatment of comorbidities** — sleep apnoea, chronic kidney disease, diabetes, anaemia and iron deficiency.
> `UNVERIFIED — agents, doses and current Australian PBS eligibility for SGLT2 inhibitors in HFpEF, which may lag the evidence.`

**Ix:** As per 0.2, with particular attention to **diastolic parameters and estimated filling pressures on echocardiography** (*why:* required for the diagnosis; *what:* E/e′, atrial size, pulmonary pressures). **Assessment for amyloid where the phenotype fits** (*why:* treatable and increasingly recognised; *what:* voltage-mass discordance, bone scintigraphy, cardiac MRI). Exercise or stress echocardiography where resting studies are equivocal (*why:* filling pressures may only rise on exertion, so a normal resting study does not exclude HFpEF; *what:* exercise-induced rise in filling pressures).

### 0.4.1 Mx – Immediate
Cautious diuresis for congestion — **these patients tip into hypovolaemia and prerenal failure more readily than HFrEF patients.**

### 0.4.2 Mx – Definitive
SGLT2 inhibitor, blood pressure control, AF management, weight loss, exercise, comorbidity treatment.

### 0.4.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
See 0.7.

---

## 0.5 Decompensation — Precipitants and the Chronic-Acute Interface

> [!tip] Always look for the precipitant — decompensation rarely happens spontaneously
> **· Non-adherence** — to medication, fluid restriction or dietary sodium. **The commonest precipitant, and it is best explored non-judgementally: cost, side effects, complexity, health literacy, and simply running out.**
> **· Infection** — respiratory or urinary.
> **· Ischaemia or infarction** — which may be silent.
> **· Arrhythmia, particularly new atrial fibrillation** — the loss of atrial contribution and the rapid rate together decompensate a marginal ventricle.
> **· Anaemia and iron deficiency.**
> **· Drugs — NSAIDs above all, plus corticosteroids, calcium channel blockers, and newly started chemotherapy.**
> **· Uncontrolled hypertension.**
> **· Thyroid dysfunction.**
> **· Pulmonary embolism.**
> **· Excess salt, fluid or alcohol** — a holiday, a funeral, a restaurant meal.
> **· Worsening renal function.**
> **Treating the decompensation without identifying the precipitant guarantees readmission.**

> [!tip] The wet-dry / warm-cold framework
> A practical bedside classification that directs treatment:
> | | **Warm (adequately perfused)** | **Cold (hypoperfused)** |
> |---|---|---|
> | **Dry (no congestion)** | Compensated — optimise oral therapy | Hypovolaemic or low output — cautious fluid, consider inotrope |
> | **Wet (congested)** | **The commonest presentation — diurese** | **Cardiogenic shock — inotropes, ICU** — cross-refer [[F0.3]] 0.3 |
> **Congestion** is assessed by JVP, orthopnoea, oedema, hepatojugular reflux, weight gain and **bendopnoea** (breathlessness on bending forward to tie shoes, a useful and under-asked symptom of raised filling pressures).
> **Perfusion** is assessed by peripheral temperature, pulse pressure, mental state, and urine output.

> [!warning] Diuresis, and diuretic resistance
> Intravenous loop diuretic, with **daily weights, strict fluid balance, and monitoring of renal function and potassium.**
> **Diuretic resistance** — inadequate response despite escalating loop diuretic — is managed by **sequential nephron blockade: adding a thiazide-type diuretic to block distal compensatory sodium reabsorption.** This is effective and requires close electrolyte monitoring because it can cause profound hypokalaemia and hyponatraemia. `UNVERIFIED — agents, doses and monitoring frequency.`
> **Do not stop the disease-modifying drugs during decompensation unless there is a specific reason** (hypotension, hyperkalaemia, acute kidney injury). Beta-blockers in particular are frequently stopped and never restarted, and **continuing them where possible is associated with better outcomes.**

> [!danger] The post-discharge period is the highest-risk time
> **Readmission rates after a heart failure admission are high, and the first weeks are the most vulnerable.**
> **Early review — within about a week of discharge — reduces readmission**, and should be arranged before the patient leaves, not left to them to organise.
> Ensure at discharge: **the precipitant has been identified and addressed · the medications are optimised and the changes explained · a follow-up appointment exists · weights and an action plan are in place · and the GP has an accurate discharge summary stating what changed and why.**

Acute pulmonary oedema management is in [[F0.5]] 0.3 and is not repeated here — the key points being **sit up, oxygen, non-invasive ventilation and nitrates**, with diuretics playing a smaller role than commonly assumed in the hypertensive "SCAPE" presentation.

**Ix:** **Daily weight and strict fluid balance** (*why:* the most reliable measure of diuretic response, and more reliable than fluid charts, which are notoriously inaccurate; *what:* weight trend). UEC and potassium daily during active diuresis (*why:* acute kidney injury and electrolyte derangement; *what:* creatinine, potassium, sodium). **ECG and troponin** (*why:* ischaemia and arrhythmia as precipitants; *what:* ischaemic change, AF). FBC, iron studies, TFTs, CRP (*why:* anaemia, iron deficiency, thyroid dysfunction and infection as precipitants; *what:* the cause). CXR (*why:* congestion and infection; *what:* oedema, consolidation). **Medication history including over-the-counter NSAIDs** (*why:* a common and entirely reversible precipitant that patients do not report unless asked specifically; *what:* NSAID use).

### 0.5.1 Mx – Immediate
Oxygen, sit up, IV diuretic, identify and treat the precipitant, and NIV plus nitrates in acute pulmonary oedema.

### 0.5.2 Mx – Definitive
Optimise the four pillars before discharge, and address the precipitant.

### 0.5.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
Early follow-up, action plan, and heart failure service involvement.

---

## 0.6 Comorbidities, Devices and Advanced Heart Failure

> [!tip] The comorbidities that change outcomes
> **· Atrial fibrillation** — very common and mutually reinforcing. **Catheter ablation in selected patients with HFrEF and AF improves outcomes** and is worth considering rather than accepting permanent rate control. Cross-refer [[B3]] 0.4.
> **· Iron deficiency** — treat with **intravenous** iron; oral absorption is poor in heart failure.
> **· Chronic kidney disease** — cardiorenal syndrome, where each worsens the other. The temptation to stop RAAS blockade to protect the kidneys usually harms both.
> **· Sleep-disordered breathing** — common, and treating obstructive sleep apnoea helps.
> **· Depression** — common, under-treated, and associated with worse adherence and outcomes. Ask about it.
> **· Diabetes, COPD, obesity, frailty and cognitive impairment**, each of which complicates management and self-care.

> [!info] Devices
> **· Implantable cardioverter defibrillator (ICD)** — for **primary prevention of sudden cardiac death in HFrEF with a persistently low ejection fraction despite an adequate period of optimal medical therapy.** The key point is **"despite optimal therapy for an adequate period"** — EF frequently improves on the four pillars, and assessing eligibility too early leads to unnecessary implantation.
> **· Cardiac resynchronisation therapy (CRT)** — biventricular pacing for patients with **left bundle branch block and a broad QRS**, which resynchronises a dyssynchronous ventricle. It improves symptoms, ejection fraction and survival in appropriately selected patients.
> `UNVERIFIED — EF thresholds, QRS duration criteria, the required duration of optimal medical therapy, and NYHA class requirements for both devices.`
> **· ICD deactivation must be discussed as part of end-of-life planning** — an unsuspended device will shock a dying patient repeatedly. Cross-refer [[B3]] 0.6 and [[GER2]] 0.5.

> [!warning] Recognising advanced heart failure and when to refer
> Features suggesting advanced disease: **recurrent hospitalisations · persistent NYHA III–IV symptoms despite optimal therapy · inability to tolerate the disease-modifying drugs because of hypotension or renal impairment · escalating diuretic requirements · progressive renal dysfunction · hyponatraemia · weight loss and cardiac cachexia · frequent ICD shocks · and declining functional capacity.**
> **These patients should be referred to a specialist heart failure service for consideration of advanced therapies — mechanical circulatory support, transplantation, or a palliative approach — and the referral is frequently made too late.**
> Advanced therapies include **left ventricular assist devices** and **cardiac transplantation**, with strict eligibility criteria and limited availability. **Continuous inotrope infusion has a role as a palliative measure** for symptom control in patients not eligible for advanced therapies, with the explicit understanding that it does not prolong life.

**Ix:** Repeat echocardiography after optimisation (*why:* determines device eligibility; *what:* EF). ECG for QRS duration and morphology (*why:* CRT eligibility; *what:* LBBB, QRS width). Right heart catheterisation in advanced disease (*why:* haemodynamic assessment for transplant and device candidacy; *what:* filling pressures, cardiac output, pulmonary vascular resistance). Cardiopulmonary exercise testing (*why:* objective functional assessment and transplant prognostication; *what:* peak VO₂). Frailty and cognitive assessment (*why:* determines suitability for burdensome therapies; *what:* frailty grade — cross-refer [[GER1]] 0.2).

### 0.6.1 Mx – Immediate
Not applicable.

### 0.6.2 Mx – Definitive
Device implantation, advanced therapy referral, or transition to a palliative approach — with the decision made explicitly and with the patient.

### 0.6.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
Device follow-up and deactivation planning.

---

## 0.7 Self-Management, Prognosis and Palliative Care

> [!tip] The self-management package
> **· Daily weights, at the same time, on the same scales, recorded** — with **a written action plan specifying what weight gain over what period should trigger what action** (an extra diuretic dose, or contacting the heart failure nurse or GP). **This is the single most practical self-management tool and it prevents admissions.** `UNVERIFIED — the weight gain thresholds used in Australian action plans.`
> **· Sodium restriction** — sensible moderation rather than severe restriction; the evidence for aggressive restriction is weaker than traditionally taught and it can worsen appetite and nutrition.
> **· Fluid restriction** — **individualised, not universal.** Blanket fluid restriction for all heart failure patients is not supported and causes thirst, poor adherence and hypovolaemia. It has a role in hyponatraemia and refractory congestion.
> **· Medication adherence** — explore cost, complexity, side effects and understanding.
> **· Exercise and cardiac rehabilitation.**
> **· Vaccination** — influenza, pneumococcal and COVID-19, which reduce decompensation from infection.
> **· Alcohol moderation or abstinence**, and smoking cessation.
> **· Recognising deterioration** — increasing breathlessness, orthopnoea, weight gain, oedema, and reduced exercise tolerance.

> [!info] Multidisciplinary heart failure programs work
> **Nurse-led heart failure programs and multidisciplinary disease management reduce readmission and mortality**, through education, titration support, early detection of decompensation and rapid access.
> They are established in Australia and are **under-referred.** Referral to a heart failure service or program is a concrete action with good evidence behind it.

> [!danger] The prognosis is worse than many cancers, and patients are rarely told
> **Mortality after a heart failure hospitalisation is substantial, and five-year survival in advanced heart failure compares unfavourably with several common cancers.**
> **Patients and families consistently overestimate prognosis**, in part because the illness trajectory is one of exacerbation and partial recovery, which reads as "getting better again" each time.
> **This is why advance care planning in heart failure is systematically neglected** — there is never an obviously terminal moment. Cross-refer [[GER2]] 0.5 for the organ-failure trajectory.

> [!tip] Palliative care in heart failure
> **Early integration alongside active treatment**, not as a terminal handover.
> Symptom priorities: **breathlessness (opioids are effective and under-used in refractory breathlessness in heart failure)**, fatigue, pain, depression and anxiety.
> **Practical end-of-life issues specific to heart failure: ICD deactivation, rationalising the medication burden, deciding about further hospitalisation, and preferred place of care.**
> **The conversations to have while the patient is well: what matters most to them, what trade-offs they would accept, who speaks for them, and what they would not want.** Cross-refer [[A10]] 0.3 and [[GER2]] 0.6.

**Ix:** Regular review of weight, symptoms, NYHA class, renal function, electrolytes and adherence (*why:* the ongoing measures that detect deterioration and permit titration; *what:* trends). **Depression screening** (*why:* common, under-treated, worsens outcomes; *what:* depressive symptoms). Frailty and functional assessment in older patients (*why:* informs the intensity of therapy and the goals of care; *what:* frailty and function).

### 0.7.1 Mx – Immediate
Not applicable.

### 0.7.2 Mx – Definitive
Structured self-management education, action plan, and heart failure program referral.

### 0.7.3 Mx – Chronic/long-term
Regular review, honest prognostic discussion, advance care planning, and palliative care integration as the disease advances.

---

> [!note] Cross-references
> Acute pulmonary oedema and SCAPE → [[F0.5]] 0.3 · Cardiogenic shock and mechanical complications → [[F0.3]] 0.3 · Chest pain and troponin interpretation → [[B1]] · Hypertension → [[B2]] · Atrial fibrillation, devices and ICD deactivation → [[B3]] 0.4, 0.6 · Syncope in heart failure → [[B4]] · Murmurs, valvular disease and peripartum cardiomyopathy → [[B5]] 0.1, 0.3 · Oedema and its differential → [[B6]] 0.1–0.2 · Calcium channel blocker classes → [[F0.1]] 0.7 · Euglycaemic DKA on SGLT2 inhibitors → [[F0.2]] 0.3 · Immune checkpoint inhibitor myocarditis → [[A5]] 0.7 · Anaemia and iron deficiency → [[J1]] · Frailty → [[GER1]] 0.2 · End-of-life trajectories and advance care planning → [[GER2]] 0.5–0.6 · Rheumatic heart disease → [[F0.5]] 0.10 and [[AU1]]
